Turning a working deployment into a failing one to gain a guarantee it may not need is the wrong default, so AUTO enables it where support is known (`ANTHROPIC`, `OLLAMA`, `OPENROUTER`) and `ON` is the opt-in for a gateway whose operator knows better. Either way `OpenAiCompatibleLlmClient` **degrades on its own**: a 4xx on a schema-carrying request earns one unconstrained retry, and the refusal is then remembered. **Remembered against the model, not the client**, which is what lets `OPENROUTER` be in the AUTO set at all: it is the one provider here whose answer to "do you support schemas" is per *model* — one base URL and one key route to hundreds of them, and only some of them, served by only some upstream providers, implement `response_format`. A single flag per client meant one schema-less model disabled constrained decoding for every model chosen afterwards, silently and for the client's whole lifetime, since `LlmClientProvider` fingerprints provider, base URL and key and the model is in none of them. Per model, a refusal costs one extra request for that model and nothing for the next. Without the retry at all, an operator would be told their gateway is broken when it merely lacks a field.
- `LlmUsage` — **what a call actually cost**, in tokens *and in money*, carried on `LlmResponse` and out through `ProcessMiningResult.usage` and the live `ANALYSIS_USAGE` event. `costUsd` is **read, never derived**: OpenRouter prices every response and that figure was being dropped — on the provider now shipped by default, which bills per token — while no price table lives in this application, so a figure on screen is one a provider stood behind. It follows the same nullability rule as the tokens and needs it more: a provider that prices nothing (the OpenAI API, Ollama, SpectraLLM) reports `null`, never `0`, since `0.0` is a real measurement (a free model). **`reasoningTokens` is the symmetric breakdown on the output side** (`completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`), and it is a breakdown rather than an addition — the deliberation is already inside `outputTokens`, so what it buys is the *explanation* of a cost, not the cost. It matters here because this application meets reasoning models routinely: `LlmJsonSupport` exists to strip their traces, and `hasUnterminatedReasoning` already reports a model that spent its whole output budget thinking and never reached the JSON — but only after that run failed. The figure shows the same budget being eaten on a run that *succeeded*, in time to raise `claude.max-tokens` or change model. Its nullability reads the other way round from `cachedInputTokens`: `0` is the ordinary case (a model that did not deliberate) and only `null` means nobody counted, which is why the UI shows it solely when it is above zero. The browser's `totalCostUsd` refuses a session total as soon as one call was unpriced — the partial sum would understate a real bill with the assurance of an exact one — and `formatCostUsd` drops to six decimals under a cent, two decimals having printed `$0.00` on every analysis. Nothing measured this before, which meant every tuning decision in the ingestion pipeline — `prompt-char-budget`, the per-topic sample, the digest caps — rested on reasoning rather than on a number, and nobody could say what a snapshot cost. **Token counts are boxed and `null` means "not reported"**: SpectraLLM returns no accounting at all and a lean gateway may omit `usage`, and a zero there would say the call was free — the same class of lie the previous audit removed elsewhere. `durationMs` is measured on our side, so it is real even when the tokens are unknown. A *failed* analysis carries its usage too (`ProcessMiningResult.failed(message, usage)`): a run that times out still burned tokens, and hiding that would make a repeatedly-failing session look free.
- `LlmClientProvider` — **the only way to obtain an `LlmClient`**, and the reason `LlmClientFactory` is called from exactly one place. `POST /api/config` repoints the LLM at runtime, but the two services that call the model resolved their client in their constructor: what they read per call (base URL, model) followed a change, while what the client *carries* — the provider chosen by the factory, and the API key baked into the Anthropic SDK client — did not. Switching Ollama → Anthropic in Settings therefore left every analysis on Ollama, and `POST /api/config/test-llm` built a client of its own and cheerfully reported the new provider reachable: a settings page confirming a change the engine ignores. The provider rebuilds only when a fingerprint of `provider|baseUrl|hash(apiKey)` moves, so the common path is a volatile read rather than a new HTTP client (and its connection pool) per request; `test-llm` now goes through it too, since the point of that endpoint is to prove what the analyses will use. Both services hold a `Supplier<LlmClient>`, which is also the test seam.
- `FieldProfilingService` — sends Kafka message samples to the configured model for semantic field detection (CORRELATION_ID / TIMESTAMP / STATUS / AMOUNT); returns `FieldProfileResult`. Profiles from digests (structures + a few example values per path, `maxSampleFields = maxShapePaths`), not from raw payloads. Never swallow exceptions — propagate so callers surface the real error. **Its prompt budget is global, like the analysis prompt's**, which it was not: it sized itself `max(4 000, prompt-char-budget / topics)` *per topic* with no ceiling, the exact arithmetic corrected in `LlmAnalysisService.appendMessages` and left standing in the sibling — so "Select all" on a hundred-topic cluster claimed 400 000 characters against a budget of 120 000, in the one prompt that runs on every pipeline whether or not an analysis follows. A global remainder now governs how many topics are detailed, and the ones past it are named in the prompt (with an instruction not to invent mappings for them) rather than dropped in silence. **What the read could not cover is named to the operator too** (`SnapshotRead.scopeNotes()`, prepended to `warnings`, which `SchemaValidationPanel` already renders): a topic that never resolved and a topic the model looked at and had nothing to say about both produce no row in the validation panel, so without the note they are the same screen — and the notes go *first* because they explain an absence the model cannot know about, never having been shown the topic.
  **A profiling run that did not happen is not one that found nothing** (`FieldProfileResult.error`, and the two `failed()` factories beside it). Every failure path — no API key, an unreachable endpoint, an answer that would not parse — answered 200 with an empty `topics` list and the reason in `warnings`, which is the *same shape* as a genuine profiling of topics that hold no messages. The two send an operator to opposite places: one is a cluster to go and look at, the other an endpoint, a model or a key to go and fix. It is the distinction `ProcessMiningResult.error` already draws for the analysis half of this pipeline, and its absence from the profiling half cost real time in the session that fixed `KafkaSnapshotReader` — three model failures were read as failed Kafka reads and chased through two rebuilds of the consumer, because "sampled 0/6" meant both things. When `error` is set nothing else in the record is a finding and the page returns to the topic selection with that message; a run that profiled nothing *without* an error keeps its own wording, which now says to check that the selected topics hold messages rather than sending the operator back to the LLM settings. The usage still travels where it is known — a run that burned tokens and then failed to parse is not a free one — and both exception paths report through `SqlErrorClassifier.explain`, which is never blank where `getMessage()` is null for an NPE. **And the model does not get to set it**: `error` is a record component and the model's own JSON is bound into that record with `FAIL_ON_UNKNOWN_PROPERTIES` disabled, so a model volunteering an `"error"` key beside twelve good profiles had its run reported as a hard failure and the profiles thrown away — the converse of the confusion the field was added to remove, and one word of the answer undoing the rest of it. It carries `@JsonProperty(access = READ_ONLY)`, the same annotation and the same reasoning as `usage` beside it: `error` means *this run did not happen*, which is a fact about the call and never something the answer may assert about itself. What the model wanted to say survives in `warnings`, which is the field the prompt asks for it by name.
- `ProcessModelBuilder` + `ProcessModel` — **the process, measured.** Four of the five entries in `AuditPromptCatalog` are questions about a *case* (ordering, orphans, latency, duplicates are each "par correlation id"), and `LlmAnalysisService` sampled its messages **per topic, independently, on offset order** — so whether one case survived in two topics' samples at once was an accident of those topics carrying the same cases, in the same order, at comparable volume, which stops being true the moment a pipeline branches, filters or retries. On the seeded demo cluster the overlap is nil by construction: `demo.payments.*` / `demo.shipments.*` are correlated to the orders **by header only** and never carry the order id in the payload. What a small model can still do with such a sample is infer a plausible pipeline from the **topic names**, and nothing in the answer distinguishes that from an observation. `PayloadDigest.fields()` already carried the case id, the timestamp and the status per record — that is an event log, and nothing ever grouped by the first column. The builder does: directly-follows edges with occurrence counts and p50/p95/max latency, variants (distinct end-to-end paths) with case counts, start/end distributions, repeated `(case, activity)` pairs, and the case ids to inline as worked examples. Pure, so two runs over one window produce the same model, and pinned by `ProcessModelBuilderTest` rather than judged by reading an answer. Four things are load-bearing. **The activity is the topic**, except on a topic the operator mapped a *status* for — mapping one is a deliberate statement that the status is what moves, which is the event-sourced shape — and that is **bounded**: past `max-status-activities` a "status" is not the closed set a status is, so the topic collapses back to one node and the model is *told* which topic that happened to. **There is no notion of a terminal activity and nothing is called an orphan**: which activity ought to end a process is a business fact this application does not have, and deriving it from the data is circular, so what is reported is the *distribution* of end activities and the reading is left to the model. **The window boundary is stated**, because a snapshot manufactures incomplete cases at both ends — a case whose first event predates the read is missing its start, one still running when the read stopped is missing its end, neither is a defect and both look exactly like one. And **not available is a state**: with no mapping there is no case id and therefore no event log, reported as `available = false` with a reason rather than as a process with zero cases — same rule as `SnapshotRead` and the activity series. **A record whose payload broke off mid-parse is not a case**, which the eval found the hard way: a streaming parser reads the fields it reaches, `id` is usually the first of them, so each of the two truncated records `setup-demo.sh` plants inside `demo.orders.3.enriched` arrived carrying a correlation id and became a one-event case that *ended* at enrichment — a pipeline stalling at its third stage, reported where a producer had written two bad records, and `ORD-666` nominated as a spotlight case and inlined into the prompt as a worked example. They are outside the log now, and the exclusion is **counted and named** in `notes` rather than done in silence: dropping records without saying so is the mirror defect of counting them, and it is named apart from "carried no correlation id", since a mapping that does not cover a topic and a producer writing corrupt bytes send an operator to different places. The audit stays where a corrupt payload is a finding in its own right. A backwards hop cannot surface as a negative duration (the log is sorted by event time), so what is counted instead is `outOfOrderCount`: pairs the broker saw in the opposite order to their business timestamps, which is what a skewed producer or a back-dated event looks like.
- `EventTime` — the one place a field value is resolved to epoch millis (below 10^10 is seconds, then ISO-8601, then a space-separated local date-time read as UTC; `null` means *unresolvable*, never the epoch). It was written twice identically, and the second copy said so — `FlinkSqlService.parseEventTimeMillis` carried the comment "mirroring the metric engine" above a body byte-equivalent to `MetricService.toEpochMillis` — with a third file keeping the threshold on its own. Both now delegate. Same argument as `SecureXml` and `LogSafe`.
- **Process Mining runs without an LLM, for the half that never needed one.** `isApiKeyMissing()` was checked *before* the read, so a deployment with no key configured got nothing at all — while the directly-follows graph, the variants and the latencies are counting over records this side already holds. The key is checked after the read and the measurement now: a run with no model reports the `ProcessModel` it built, with `error` naming what is missing (the flowchart, the narrative and the anomalies, which is the half a model was for) and, when there is no field mapping either, saying so too — those send an operator to two different screens. It is the same rule `coverage` already followed on a failed analysis: `usage`, `coverage` and `processModel` are measurements taken on *this* side of the call, so losing the call cannot invalidate them, and all three are `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` so a model can never assert its own scope, its own bill or its own process.
- **The measured process is on screen, not only in the prompt** (`ProcessMiningResult.processModel` → `components/processmining/ProcessModelPanel.tsx`, wording in `pages/processModel.ts`). It was computed and only the model saw it, so an operator read a *narrative* about a directly-follows graph they had no way to check — the opposite of what this application does everywhere else (Stream Flow's evidence table, the coverage notice, the evidence attached to every proposed KPI). The panel renders the transitions with p50/p95/max, the variants with their case shares, where cases ended, repeated steps and the limits of the measurement; a transition the broker saw in the opposite order to its business timestamps is marked `clock skew`, which is a finding about the estate rather than a defect to smooth away. **Unavailable renders as the reason, never as rows of zeros** — and it is what the page shows when there is no LLM configured at all. `slowestEdge` returns null below two transitions, the same rule Stream Flow's chain follows: "the slowest" says nothing when there is only one.
- `LlmAnalysisService` — generates Mermaid flowcharts + `AnomalyReport` list from correlated messages (snapshot and live modes via `analyzeSnapshot` / `analyzeLiveDigests`; `analyzeLive(List<KafkaMessage>)` digests first — the erasure clash is why the digest entry point has its own name). The prompt opens with **PROCESSUS MESURÉ** — the `ProcessModel` above, computed over *every* record read, so that part of the evidence is immune to the sampling that follows it — and the instructions tell the model to draw the flowchart from the listed transitions rather than from the topic names, since an arc the measurement does not show does not exist however obvious it looks. What is inlined after it is **whole case traces**, one per selected variant (`CAS DÉTAILLÉS`), labelled as worked examples rather than a representative sample: the proportions are in the variants section, computed over everything. A topic no spotlight case passes through is **named**, so its silence is not read as a finding about it. Where no event log could be built the section says so and **forbids the inference** — a prompt that merely omitted the flows is one the model fills in for itself — and the old per-topic `evenSample()` path is what still runs there, since describing the topics is all that is on offer. Both paths share one **STRUCTURES DE PAYLOAD** section, emitted for exactly the records that follow it (the body is built into a buffer first, so the shapes cannot describe a different sample). `sample` is capped at `max-sample-fields-in-prompt` per message with the remainder counted in `sampleOmitted`: the digest keeps forty because profiling aggregates them per path across a topic, but inlining forty *per message* spent a topic's whole share on the values the mapping did **not** name. Escaping is single-pass — chaining `String.replace()` copied every payload four times.
  **A failure is reported as one**: `ProcessMiningResult.error` is what separates a refusal from a result. Every failure path — no API key, an unreachable provider, an unparseable answer — used to put its message in `comments` and answer 200, so the page rendered "LLM call failed: Connection refused" inside its *Analysis Commentary* panel, under an empty diagram, exactly as it renders a real narrative. When `error` is set nothing else in the record is a finding, the page shows an error instead of a Results step, and `KafkaLiveConsumer` emits **`ANALYSIS_ERROR`** rather than passing the reason off as commentary — an event the server had always been able to send and the page had no listener for, so a live window whose analysis failed left the UI looking healthy (connected, counters ticking) with a flowchart that simply never arrived. A parse failure on an answer that stops mid-object names `claude.max-tokens`, because hitting the output cap is the commonest cause and the setting is the fix.
  **The prompt budget is global, not per topic × a floor.** The per-topic share keeps a 2 000-character floor (a topic allotted 200 characters contributes nothing an analysis can use), but a floor multiplied by the topic count is not a budget: at 2 000 apiece, 100 topics claim 200 000 against a 120 000 `prompt-char-budget`, and the ceiling the whole digest pipeline exists to enforce was quietly exceeded by the section that inlines the messages. A global remainder now governs how many topics get their share, and the ones past it are named in the prompt rather than dropped in silence.
  **And the answer states what it was able to look at** (`ProcessMiningResult.coverage`, a `ProcessMiningCoverage` of `TopicCoverage` rows). A flowchart and a list of anomalies cannot say what they rest on, and three things reduce the scope without saying so: topics that hold nothing, topics that do not resolve on the cluster, and — the one nothing could see — topics read in full whose records never fit the character budget, which was named *in the prompt*, to the model, and nowhere else. A run over eight topics where two never reached the model rendered exactly like a run over eight, so the model's silence about them read as a finding **about** them rather than as the absence of a question, which is the strongest way this feature can mislead. Each row carries **three** numbers, and the third was added because the measured process broke the other two: `messagesRead`, `messagesMeasured` (records that entered the event log, so they bear on every transition, variant and latency whether or not any of them is shown) and `messagesDetailed` (records inlined verbatim — a worked case trace, or the per-topic sample where no event log could be built). Folding the last two into one `messagesAnalysed` reported **"6 of 3,000"** about a run whose aggregate covered all three thousand, and sent the reader to raise a prompt budget that was 6 % spent — measured on a six-topic, 3 000-record fixture. A **total** `messagesMeasured` of zero is the exact signal that no event log was built and the per-topic sampling ran instead, which is what lets `processMiningCoverage.ts` pick the right sentence without a second flag to keep in step: on that path a topic read and never shown is the budget case, on the measured path it is a topic no record of which carried the mapped correlation id — two causes with two different repairs, and raising the budget fixes neither of the second. `readable` false is a name that resolves to nothing here, never an empty topic; totals are derived from the rows, so the header cannot disagree with them. Deliberately **structured rather than sentences** — `pages/processMiningCoverage.ts` does the wording, in one place — with `warnings` left for what the rows cannot express, such as the field mapping the controller could not resolve. It travels on a **failed** analysis too: a run that read four hundred messages and then lost the model still knows what it read, and that is what sizes the next attempt. The live path carries none of it, its scope being already reported per window by `WINDOW_STATS`.
  **A read that came back with nothing is not a question worth asking.** Both this service and the profiling one used to build a prompt of headings and pay for an answer about the absence of data — an invented pipeline, or a mapping proposal about topics nobody had shown the model, which step 3 then invites the operator to validate. The reason is stated instead, and `SnapshotRead.emptyReadExplanation()` names which of the three empties it was. The two halves report it differently, on the rule each record was written for: the analysis has no answer at all, so it sets `ProcessMiningResult.error`; for profiling, topics that merely hold nothing is a finding about the cluster — where the page already sends the operator — and only a **failed read** sets `FieldProfileResult.error`.
- `KafkaSnapshotReader` — temp KafkaConsumer (group `snapshot-reader-{uuid}`, `enable.auto.commit=false`) supporting EARLIEST / LATEST_N / TIMESTAMP seek modes. `read()` returns raw `KafkaMessage`s; `readDigested()` uses `ByteArrayDeserializer` and digests each record as it arrives, so nothing accumulates — that is the one both LLM services use. Fetch sizes come from `process-mining.*`. **Three separate faults made a multi-topic snapshot read a random subset of what it was asked for**, and the symptom was identical each time: Process Mining profiled one topic of six and reported the rest as empty, an analysis then concluding in as many words that only one topic contained messages. None of them is visible from outside — a snapshot that read nothing and a cluster holding nothing are the same empty list — so the three were found one behind the other, each hiding the next.
  - **`seekToLatestN` clamped to `0` instead of the partition's beginning offset.** This is the root cause and the one that survives every retry: retention deletes the oldest segments, so on a trimmed topic offset 0 no longer exists, seeking there is out of range, the consumer applies `auto.offset.reset` — **`latest`** in this mode — and the position jumps to the **end** of the log. Nothing is delivered and a full topic reads as an empty one. On the demo cluster, after the seed data had aged out and been reseeded, five of six topics came back empty and the sixth was the only one whose log still began at 0. `KafkaAdminService` already followed this rule for its recent-record seeks — it is documented a few paragraphs above — and this class did not.
  - **Termination was decided by an empty poll** (`if (records.isEmpty()) break;`). A fresh consumer's first poll very often returns nothing while metadata resolves and the assignment settles; read as "these topics are exhausted", it made the outcome non-deterministic — three runs of one identical request sampled 1 topic, then 0, then 6. Same defect `drain()` was fixed for.
  - **And the offsets it was then made to steer by were `consumer.position(tp)`, which is not a consumption watermark.** It is the *fetch* position: the client prefetches in the background, across every assigned partition, and advances it as responses are buffered rather than as records are returned. Measured against the demo broker, one poll delivered 2 records of one topic while `position()` reported the log end for all eighteen partitions, twenty-odd records still sitting undelivered — so the read stopped after a single poll, exactly reproducing the symptom the empty-poll fix had just removed. `drain()` compares the same thing and is single-topic, which is what has been masking it there.

  What the loop does now: a **per-partition cursor of its own**, seeded from the seek position before any poll and advanced only by records the handler was actually given, compared against end offsets taken once up front. `hasUnreadOffsets` is pure and never asks the consumer anything. It also consults the **per-topic cap** — once a topic has its quota its partitions stop keeping the loop alive, or a busy topic filled in the first poll would hold the read open for a quiet one already finished — and a partition with no known end offset counts as read, since turning a missing entry into an unbounded read is the opposite of the bounding. A **5 s silence budget** and the 30 s wall-clock budget remain as nets against a broker that stopped answering, never as the normal exit; the silence net is expressed in time rather than as a count of empty polls, because what makes it safe to stop is that nothing has arrived for a while and a count only means that through whatever the poll timeout happens to be. **What restarts that clock is a record the read *kept*, not one it was merely handed**, and the partitions of a topic that has its quota are `pause()`d. Those two go together and both were missing: a capped topic went on being fetched to its end offsets, every record of it discarded at the cap check — and each of those polls counted as activity, so the net could never fire in the one shape it exists for. A busy topic beside a partition whose leader has gone away held the read open for the full 30 s wall clock on the Process Mining hot path, spending the rest of the fetch bandwidth on records nobody would keep. Nothing is resumed: the consumer is closed at the end of the read, and a topic that has its quota does not stop having it. `aFloodOfRecordsItIsDiscardingDoesNotKeepTheReadAlive` pins it, and measures 30 000 ms against the code it describesAnd the read says at DEBUG what it is about to do** — partitions assigned, how many hold records, the `start..end` of each — then what it collected. Its absence is what made the three causes indistinguishable: every one of them ended at the top of the loop, the one exit with nothing to say, so three rebuild-and-observe cycles went into re-deriving what one line states. It is also what finally separated a failed *read* from a failed *analysis*: with the reader fixed, a profiling run still reported five of six topics as unprofiled, and the log showed all sixteen records collected in 30 ms — the empty answer was the model returning a body with no content at all, having spent its output budget reasoning. A read that comes back empty must be able to say which of those it was. `createConsumer()` is the test seam (`KafkaSnapshotReaderTest` drives a `MockConsumer`); each defect test was verified to fail against the code it describes before the fix landed, and the prefetch one has to make the mock **lie the way a real client lies** — report the log end — rather than throw, since a throw is caught by the reader's own error path and would pass against the very code it exists to fail against.

  **A fourth fault sat one line above all three, and cost more than any of them: a topic the broker describes no partition for used to cost the whole read.** `partitionsFor` answers `null` for a topic that does not exist, `.stream()` on it threw, and the catch below returned an empty list *for every topic in the request* — so one deleted topic, or one typo in a selection of eight, produced a profiling run and an analysis of nothing at all, logged as an error nobody reads and rendered as a cluster with no messages in it. Such a topic now costs itself, is named at WARN, and the read goes on; a request naming only unknown topics returns immediately rather than spending the silence budget polling an empty assignment.

  **And the read says what it could not do** (`readSnapshot` → `SnapshotRead`; `readDigested` is the same read with the answer thrown away, kept for callers that only want records). A short list has three meanings that send the reader to three different places — the topic holds nothing, the topic does not resolve here, the read stopped on its own budget with records still waiting — and an empty list says none of them. It is the rule the consumer-lag and activity work was done for, on the one read that had been left with a bare list: `messagesByTopic` keys **every requested topic**, so a caller can tell an absent row from a zero; `unreadableTopics` names what did not resolve; `readError` and `budgetExhausted` say the counts are partial. `emptyReadExplanation()` and `scopeNotes()` live on the record rather than in either service, because both callers had been about to write the same three sentences.
- `KafkaLiveConsumer` — sliding window consumer; triggers LLM on window fill (default 100 msg) or timeout (default 30s); pushes via `SseEmitterManager`. **Buffers digests, never payloads**: values are consumed as `byte[]` and digested on the poll thread, so a window of a hundred 1 MB documents costs a few hundred KB instead of hundreds of MB; the buffer is capped by count (`windowSize × 10`) *and* by estimated heap (`max-window-digest-bytes`), dropping oldest and reporting the count. Partitions are seeked to the end from a `ConsumerRebalanceListener` (not after a fixed 500 ms poll), so a backlog is skipped reliably and again after any rebalance. `WINDOW_STATS` is emitted from the poll thread when the window is cut — before the LLM runs — and carries the ingestion counters (`messagesReceived`, `bytesReceived`, `maxPayloadBytes`, `droppedMessages`, `parseFailures`, `distinctShapes`). **Threading contract**: the KafkaConsumer is only ever touched by the per-session polling task (init subscribe included); `stopSession()` just raises a stop flag + `consumer.wakeup()` and the polling task closes everything in `finishSession()`. LLM analyses run on a dedicated `analysisExecutor` pool — never on the 4-thread scheduler shared by all sessions' polling/heartbeat tasks. `createConsumer()` is the test seam (`KafkaLiveConsumerTest` drives a `MockConsumer` through it).
- `SseEmitterManager` — manages `Map<sessionId, SseEmitter>` (5 min timeout, heartbeat every 15s)
- `KraftQuorumMetrics` — polls `KafkaAdminService.getQuorumSnapshot()` (cheap, uncached single admin call) every 30s on a dedicated daemon thread and exposes Prometheus gauges: `kafka_quorum_leader_id` / `kafka_quorum_leader_epoch` / `kafka_quorum_high_watermark` and `kafka_quorum_replica_lag{replicaId,role=voter|observer}`. No gauge is registered on Zookeeper-mode clusters (null snapshot); registered gauges keep their last value across transient failures.
- `ConsumerLagMetrics` — exports consumer lag as Prometheus gauges (`kafka_consumer_group_lag{group,topic}`, `kafka_consumer_group_partitions_without_commit`, `kafka_consumer_group_assigned_members`, `kafka_consumer_group_lag_last_success_timestamp_seconds`), which is how a backlog *triggers* something instead of being noticed afterwards. The three series together say what a threshold on lag alone cannot: zero assigned members with a non-zero lag is the stalled case, and a group ignoring half the partitions has a backlog the lag does not count. **Opt-in and topic-scoped by construction**: a series per group x topic x partition is how a metrics backend gets killed, so partitions are aggregated away, the topics polled are named in `explorer.lag-metrics-topics` rather than discovered, nothing is registered (and no thread starts) when that list is empty, and `explorer.lag-metrics-max-series` freezes the exported set — the cap is logged once, since a silently truncated metric is worse than an absent one. The poll interval matches the 30 s cache behind `getTopicConsumers`; a failed refresh keeps the last value, because falling back to zero reads as "the backlog was resolved". **Three things count as a failed refresh, not one**: the call throwing, a response with `available == false` (an unreachable broker returns no groups, which acted on would publish "nobody consumes this any more"), and a single group carrying an `error` — that row's lag is zero because nothing could be read, and exported as a gauge a zero reads "caught up" and silences the very alert that should fire. **But a group that a successful read no longer returns is dropped** (`dropVanished`, run before the export so the freed slot is available to a replacement in the same pass): keeping the last value is right for a failed read and wrong for a decommissioned consumer, which otherwise exported the backlog of its last day for the life of the process, under an alert that could never clear. **And what keeps its last value is dated**: a frozen gauge is otherwise indistinguishable from a fresh one, so an alert on `lag > N` fires the same way whether the backlog is real and stuck or simply no longer measured — and the operator it wakes cannot tell. `kafka_consumer_group_lag_last_success_timestamp_seconds` is set only on a row that was actually measured, so the alert can require both (`lag > 10000 and time() - ..._last_success_timestamp_seconds < 120`). A timestamp rather than a boolean: same cardinality, and it carries *how* stale, which is the part a threshold needs. It costs a fourth series per group×topic, paid because a number nobody can date is a number nobody should act on; `admits` still counts group×topic pairs, so what `explorer.lag-metrics-max-series` bounds is unchanged. **The same backlog in *time* is exported too, opt-in** (`explorer.lag-metrics-time`, off by default): `kafka_consumer_group_lag_seconds{group,topic}`, the age of the oldest message the group has not read. Opt-in because it is the only series here that reads a *record* — one per lagging partition, per group, per refresh — where the others read metadata; a group committed at the end of every partition publishes 0 without reading anything, since caught up is a measurement. In seconds because the suffix has to mean what it says, while the `CONSUMER_TIME_LAG` metric template reports the same measurement in milliseconds like its sibling templates: one measurement, two units, each following the convention of where it lands. **And this one is removed rather than frozen when a refresh cannot measure it** — the opposite of the rule above, deliberately: a count that stopped being read is still roughly the count, while an *age* is not, because a backlog ages by construction. A frozen "3 min" against a backlog that has been growing for hours is not stale, it is false, and it gets worse every minute; absent, `absent()` sees it.
- `ClaudeConfig` — `@ConfigurationProperties(prefix="claude")`; reads `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` env var via `${ANTHROPIC_API_KEY:}`
- `MetricService` — bridges Flink SQL to Prometheus metrics via Micrometer. Supports 4 Prometheus types:
  - `GAUGE` → Micrometer `Gauge` (point-in-time value)
  - `COUNTER` → Micrometer `Counter` (service tracks delta between polls and increments accordingly)
  - `HISTOGRAM` → Micrometer `DistributionSummary` with `publishPercentileHistogram=true`
  - `SUMMARY` → Micrometer `DistributionSummary` with client-side quantiles (p50/p75/p90/p95/p99)

  Three templates sit beside raw SQL (`MetricTemplateType`): `TOPIC_COUNT_DELTA`, `TOPIC_TRANSIT_LATENCY` and **`CONSUMER_TIME_LAG` — a backlog expressed in time rather than in records**, and the only one that runs no SQL. It exists because a count is not actionable on its own: the same 4 000 messages are four seconds of traffic on one topic and four days on another, and it is the second case that wakes somebody. Neither number the KPI needs is in a payload — the position is in `__consumer_offsets` and the age is a record timestamp — so `KafkaAdminService.getConsumerTimeLag(topic, group)` reads them: committed offsets, then **end offsets last** (same ordering as the record lag, so a consumer racing the two calls can only overstate), then one consumer that seeks every *lagging* partition to its committed offset and keeps the first record each yields, in **one poll loop** rather than a read per partition. A partition committed at the end costs no read at all — caught up is a measurement. The value is the **age of the oldest waiting record** (`now − timestamp`), not `latest − committed`: a producer that stops leaves a backlog that keeps ageing, which is what "how stale is what this consumer serves" means. Bounded to 64 partitions and an 8 s budget. **A partition that could not be read is `null` with its reason, never 0** (`PartitionTimeLag`) — 0 means caught up, and a gauge saying so while nothing could be read silences the alert it exists to raise; when partitions are missing the metric still publishes (the ones that answered are real) with a `scopeNote` saying the value is a **floor, not a maximum**. The group is a **required** parameter rather than "the worst group reading this topic": that choice would move between refreshes and the series would change subject without saying so — same reason `topic` and `group` are the only labels. `aggregation` is `MAX` (default, what an alert is set on) or `AVG`.
  Metric status stays `pending` if `lastValue == null` — ensure aggregates use `AS metric_value` alias.
  `MetricConfig.createTableSql` (optional): when set, the DDL is executed before the metric SQL (useful for pre-registering a Flink table).
  Metric configs persist to `internal.metrics.config` via a shared lazy producer; startup restore reads the topic to its end offsets (an empty poll does NOT mean exhausted). Within one `refreshMetrics` cycle, identical queries (same sql/maxRows/timeout/readMode) execute once and are memoized (ThreadLocal cycle cache).
- `FieldMappingStore` — the field mappings validated in Process Mining, **persisted** to `explorer.field-mapping-topic` (`internal.field.mappings`, keyed by mapping id, so the topic is meant to be compacted). It was the only artefact this application produces by *correcting a model* and then threw away: a restart lost every mapping, the Metrics suggestions reported one they could no longer resolve, and recovering it meant replaying two model calls to re-derive something an operator had already fixed by hand — the proposal is cheap to replay, the correction is not. Both halves are best-effort and bounded, on the same rule as `MetricService`: the restore is driven by the **end offsets** (an empty poll is not an exhausted topic) under a 10 s budget, an unreadable record costs that record rather than the restore, an unknown JSON property is a field a later version added rather than a reason to lose the mapping, and a failure at startup leaves an empty store and a log line — never a boot that hangs on an unreachable broker. The write is **asynchronous** (with `max.block.ms` capped at 5 s): a validation is an interactive gesture, and holding it for a broker round trip would trade a real second for a hypothetical one. In memory it stays access-ordered and bounded to `MAX_ENTRIES` (200), so a live session's mapping is not evicted from under it by newer ones. `createProducer()` / `createConsumer()` are the test seams (`FieldMappingStoreTest` drives a `MockProducer` and a `MockConsumer`).
- `SettingsStore` + `StoredSettingsInitializer` — **what the Settings page is used to enter, kept across a restart.** `POST /api/config` mutated `KafkaConfig` and `ClaudeConfig` and wrote nothing anywhere: the one screen whose entire purpose is data entry was the only one whose input did not survive a stop, and it lost it *silently* — the page then rendered the `application.yml` values as if they were the operator's own, so a cluster repointed on Monday was quietly reading a different one on Tuesday with nothing on screen or in the log connecting the two. Four things are load-bearing. **A file, not an `internal.*` topic**, unlike every other store here, and it is not interchangeable: these settings *contain the bootstrap address*, so a save that repoints from cluster A to B has no answer to which of the two should receive it, and a boot cannot read the topic that would tell it which broker to read. It sits beside `FlinkJobStore` under `data/`. **Only the fields an operator actually entered** are taken over — `touchedFields` compares against the values in force *before* anything is applied, so a field is "entered" when it **changed**, not merely when the request carries it. That distinction is the rule rather than a refinement of it: the Settings page posts its entire form (Test connection applies before it probes), so "the fields this request carries" is every field there is, and the first click on Test connection would otherwise have frozen this release's whole configuration into the file, where a default that moves later could never reach the deployment again. A value `applyConfig` *derives* counts as entered too — switching provider fills a blank base URL, and forgetting to mark that stored the provider alone, so the next boot took the base URL from the YAML, which ships Ollama's, and pointed the new provider at the old one's endpoint. **The environment still wins**: precedence is environment variable / `-D` / command line, then the file, then the YAML — implemented by *removing* from the `PropertySource` anything the environment already names rather than by ordering it below `systemEnvironment`, which keeps the rule independent of where Spring places its config-data sources and lets the boot log **name** the fields it applied to. It is also the only way back out of a stored address pointing at a cluster that is gone. `claude.api-key` carries an `envAlias`, because `application.yml` binds it as `${ANTHROPIC_API_KEY:}` and the relaxed name `CLAUDE_API_KEY` is not what a deployment sets — without it a stored key would silently outrank the one in the environment, on a credential. **A store that could not be written is reported, never a failed save**: the settings are applied and this process is using them, and what must not be left standing is the belief that they will still be there after a restart, so the answer carries `settingsPersistedNow` / `settingsPersistenceError` and the page shows it instead of "Saved!". Credentials are written in clear, owner-only (`JsonStoreFile`), never returned by the API; `explorer.settings-store-secrets: false` omits them and **names** the fields it left out, in the save's answer and in the boot log — storing everything except the passwords otherwise keeps half the promise and keeps it silently, the connection failing for a credential nothing said had been dropped. Applied through an `ApplicationContextInitializer` registered in `Application.main`, not a bean: `KafkaConfig` validates in a `@PostConstruct` and `MetricService` / `FieldMappingStore` each open a consumer in theirs, so a bean assigning the stored address would be racing all three — a property source is read by the binder before any of them exists, and none needs a line of code for this. Registered from `main` rather than `spring.factories` so `@SpringBootTest` contexts cannot pick up a settings file left in the working directory.
- `FlinkTableStore` + `FlinkTableRestore` — **the `CREATE TABLE` statements written in the SQL editor**, replayed into Flink's in-memory catalogue after `ApplicationReadyEvent`. Losing them produced no error but a **substitution**: the hand-written definition died with the process, the next query on that name auto-registered a *generated* table under it, and the query then ran — same name, rows returned, a watermark or a chosen subset of columns or a connector option quietly gone. Auto-registration writes its DDL through `executeMutationSql` and never reaches the store, which is the distinction the whole thing rests on: a table derived from a topic is re-derived on demand, one somebody typed is not. Keyed by table name so a rewrite *replaces* (keeping both would replay the older one at boot and make the outcome depend on file order), `TEMPORARY` is refused because the word is the author scoping it themselves, and a statement that *failed* is not kept. The replay goes back through `executeSql` — same whitelist, same validator, same coordinator — since a second route into the engine is how the two come to behave differently, and it would otherwise be a way to run stored SQL the API itself refuses. **Each failure costs one table**, named at WARN: a definition that no longer works (a deleted topic, an option a Flink upgrade renamed) must not lose every definition written after it. And **`DELETE /api/query/table/{name}`** exists because this takes an escape hatch away — restarting used to be the only way to clear the catalogue — so a store that could only grow would be a worse defect than the one it fixes. **The name is resolved against what exists, never interpolated**: the request has to clear `isSafeIdentifier` first, and what then reaches the statement, the log and the store is the application's own string — Flink's catalogue entry, or the store's key — so a name matching neither drops nothing and submits no statement. The answer names which of three things happened (`DropOutcome`: `DROPPED` / `NOT_FOUND` / `REFUSED`), the same shape `CancelOutcome` uses a few methods away and for the same reason — a boolean had two states for three outcomes, so it reported "no such table" whenever the engine had refused to drop one that is plainly still in the schema browser. That ordering matters twice over: the store is consulted after Flink because a table can legitimately be in it and not in the catalogue (written by an older build, or already dropped) and must still be removable, or the boot would go on replaying a definition nobody can get rid of. **`remember` refuses a name that is not a safe identifier**, which is the guard the rest rests on — a backtick-quoted Flink identifier may hold a newline, and that name goes on to be a log line, a JSON key and the body of a `DROP TABLE`; refused where it enters, not cleaned up at each place it comes back out. Its pattern reads a quoted segment whole rather than stopping at the first space, which it used to: ``CREATE TABLE `weird name` `` was filed under the key `weird`, a different table from the one just created. The bin icon in the schema browser is the gesture, behind a confirm.
- `MetricSuggestionService` — **contextual KPIs derived from what this cluster has been observed to do** (`POST /api/metrics/suggestions`). The Metrics page knew nothing about the cluster: its four quick-start cards posed a `COUNT(*)` on the first table found, identical on a three-topic cluster and on an order pipeline. Two features do know something — the **audit** (per-topic counts, findings, and the flows it reconstructs) and **Stream Flow** (a key's real path, hop by hop) — and neither fed a metric. Each proposal carries a ready `MetricConfig` plus the reason it exists, and three rules hold it together. **No suggestion without evidence**: `evidence` is never empty and names the run, the measurement and its scope — a KPI nobody can trace back to an observation is the invented "99.98 % availability" tile the Metrics guide was stripped of, on the page that teaches measurement. **Thresholds are derived and say from what** (`thresholdBasis`): warning at 2× / critical at 4× a measured hop latency, 2×/4× a measured throughput gap floored at 1 %/5 %, 1.5×/2× an audited volume, the observed duplicate count and twice it. Where nothing was measured — a hop the audit could not correlate, an end-to-end volume ratio a single-key trace says nothing about — there is **no threshold, or a threshold that states it is a placeholder**, never a round number that looks considered. **Nothing is created**: a suggestion is a pre-filled form, because the SQL rests on an inferred key column and on an aggregate (`COUNT(DISTINCT …)`) only the Flink planner runs — both carried in `caveats`, and the preview is what settles them. A topic the audit flagged for a consumer finding gets a **`CONSUMER_TIME_LAG` KPI — the backlog in time, which is the half no count can express** — and it is the one proposal that goes and reads the cluster: the group id comes from `getTopicConsumers` (the largest readable backlog), **never parsed out of the audit's wording**, because a finding is prose and reading a name out of it is the lexical dependency this codebase keeps removing. What *is* lexical is only which topics to look at (`isConsumerFinding` matches "consumer group", the phrase all four of `consumerLagIssues`' messages are built from — matching "lag" caught one of them). It is deliberately proposed **without thresholds**: nothing has ever measured this topic in time, so any number would be the round one this panel exists not to print, and the caveat says to set it from the metric's own first readings. When the groups cannot be read, there is no card and a note says so rather than a silence that reads as "this topic needs no KPI". What the audit found but SQL cannot measure stays a **note**: the backlog in *records* is already exported from committed offsets by `ConsumerLagMetrics` (the note names `explorer.lag-metrics-topics`), and a parse failure is not something a query engine can count. The audit is read from this process, and from `internal.audit.history` otherwise so the panel survives a restart — a **RUNNING** report is refused (its topic list changes under the reader between two polls) and a stored report the current record cannot bind is reported, not silently dropped. The key column comes from the registered Flink schema when there is one (`id`, `*_id`, `correlation_id`, …) and falls back to the audit's own `id` convention **flagged as assumed**. **The panel is bounded, and the bound cuts by relevance** (`BY_RELEVANCE`, `MAX_SUGGESTIONS` = 24): the cap existed but sliced the list in *source* order — audit, then traces, then lineage, then mapping — so a pipeline edge a running job *declares* was dropped before a routine volume KPI, purely because lineage is consulted third. Proposals are now ranked by what they are about (the kind, read from the structured id, `time-lag` and `duplicates` ahead of `volume`), then by whether the thresholds were derived from a measurement, then by how few assumptions the card carries, with the source only as a tiebreak and the id last so the order is deterministic — cards must not shuffle between two identical audits when a browser-side dismissal is keyed on that id. **Marking precedes the cut**, which it did not: a proposal an existing metric already covers could take one of the 24 slots and push out a fresh one, so the panel said "already measured" on a card that had displaced the suggestion the operator did not have. And the truncation note says what it dropped, counted by kind, where it used to assert the remainder were "of the same kinds, on other topics" — an claim nothing checked and that was false as soon as the list mixed sources. A proposal an existing metric already covers is **marked, not hidden** (`alreadyConfigured` + the metric's name): "you already measure this" is an answer to the question. Ids are stable across runs (`source:kind:from>to`), which is what makes a browser-side dismissal stick when the next audit re-derives the same card, and one hop yields one card — **the measured process wins over the audit, which wins over a trace**, and that order is the whole of the precedence decision, since `deduplicate` keeps whichever source was built first. It used to be audit-first, on the reasoning that its evidence was the broader of the two then available; a directly-follows graph is broader still. **Three further sources feed it, and none of them infers anything.** *Lineage*: a running `INSERT INTO` job **declares** a pipeline edge, where the audit groups topics by their names and a trace follows one key — so each single-source job yields a gap KPI on a pair nobody had to guess, resolved by `LineageService.dependenciesOf` (shared, not re-implemented as a regex) over `getActiveJobsDetails()` rather than by building the whole graph. A **join is refused with its reason** stated in a note: "everything that entered came out" is a sentence about one source, and two inputs against one output have no ratio worth a threshold. **Bounded like every other family** (`MAX_LINEAGE_JOBS` = 12): resolving a statement is a Flink parse taken under the runtime's read lock, on every load of the Metrics page, so it cannot scale with whatever the cluster happens to be running — the cut is by start time, newest first, because `getActiveJobsDetails()` hands back a `Map.copyOf` with no order of its own and the jobs read would otherwise vary between two calls; what was not resolved is counted in a note, never silently absent. *Process Mining*: the field mapping an operator validated is the only place this application knows a topic's **real** correlation key and its status field. It mostly **improves the other cards** rather than adding its own — `KeySource` records whether a key came from the mapping, from the registered schema or from the `id` convention, and the caveat says which, so the operator knows whether the preview is a formality or the thing to check — and it adds one card per topic with a status field (`GROUP BY status`, one Prometheus series per value, no threshold: which status matters is the one thing this application cannot know). A **nested path is refused**, not turned into SQL that would fail on its first refresh. The mapping id travels from the browser's Process Mining draft (`pm:mapping`) like the traces do, and a mapping the server no longer holds — it aged out of the mapping topic, or the broker could not be read at startup — is **reported**, since the cards it would have produced are missing and silence would read as "Process Mining had nothing to say". *The measured process* is the third and the strongest: a `ProcessModel` carries a **distribution per transition** — p50, p95, worst, over every case the window held, grouped on a correlation id an operator validated by hand — where the audit has one average over a flow reconstructed from topic names and a trace has one observation. It reaches the server the way the traces do, because nothing keeps it: `pages/processModelEvidence.ts` mirrors `flowChains.ts` (versioned envelope erased rather than guessed at, seven-day expiry, three entries deduped on the **topics** rather than the activity labels, so two windows of one pipeline are one route) and the Metrics page posts **the freshest one only** — two graphs of two windows would produce two cards for one hop, settled by arrival order rather than by which measurement is better. What travels is `ProcessModelEvidence`, deliberately narrower than `ProcessModel` and for two reasons: every component is boxed, on `FlowChainEvidence`'s rule that a partial body must not fail a whole request, and the variants, the activity table and the spotlight case ids are the prompt's material — shipping them back to be ignored is data nobody needs to hold. Two families come out of it. A hop-latency KPI per transition **between two topics**, with 2×/4× the p95 as thresholds; a transition inside one topic — which is what a mapped status produces — is counted in a note instead, since the latency template correlates two topics and pointing it at one would compare a topic with itself. And a rework KPI per topic a case was seen revisiting, **deliberately with no threshold**: the measurement counts cases revisiting a step inside the window while the query counts keys sharing the topic over a bounded scan, so a number taken from one and applied to the other would only *look* derived. One constant is arithmetic rather than taste, which is why it exists at all on a panel that refuses round numbers: below **20 observations** there is no value strictly above the 95th percentile, so the p95 *is* the maximum — the figure is used either way, and the basis line names it "the worst of the N cases observed" rather than a percentile. The clock is stated too (`MIXED` / `RECORD_TIMESTAMP` add an evidence line): a transport delay and a business duration are different measurements. `MetricSuggestions.processMeasured` is carried separately from the cards it produced, because a run that measured a pipeline and suggested nothing is a different state from one that never ran, and deriving it from the proposals would erase exactly that distinction.

### Frontend

The SPA lives in `src/main/webapp/src/`. Stack: React 19 + TypeScript + Vite + Tailwind, Monaco (SQL editor), Recharts (metric charts), Mermaid (process-mining flowcharts), `lucide-react` + Material Symbols (icons), `axios` (API).

**App shell** — `App.tsx` wraps everything in `ToastProvider` + `ConfirmProvider`, renders the `Layout`, and defines the routes. **Pages are lazy-loaded** (`React.lazy` + `Suspense`, `ProgressBar` fallback) for code-splitting; unknown routes render a `NotFound` (404). `Layout` (`components/Layout.tsx`) is the shell: collapsible `Sidebar` (drawer on mobile) + `Header` + an internally-scrolling content viewport. It polls `/api/dashboard` every 30s for connection health and to feed global search (topics + Flink tables).

**The connection pill states a name and a verified address, never a version** (`components/connectionStatus.ts`, pure + tested). It rendered `explorer.cluster-name`, whose shipped value was `KRAFT 4.2`: a Kafka version asserted by every deployment without anything ever having asked the broker — in the one element whose job is to say what you are connected to — and left untouched by the runtime repoint of `POST /api/config`. Nothing overrode it: no compose stack, no Settings field, and it was absent from the documented environment variables. The label is now a display name (`Kafka cluster` by default, `EXPLORER_CLUSTER_NAME` to name an environment), and what *is* checkable travels beside it in the tooltip: `bootstrapServers`, added to `DashboardResponse` and read from the running `KafkaConfig`, so a repoint moves it. Disconnected, the tooltip names the address that did not answer — "Disconnected" alone never helped anyone diagnose anything. Before the first poll answers, the pill says `Connecting…` rather than a hardcoded name; the frontend's own fallback used to be a third value (`Docker Cluster`, against `DOCKER CLUSTER` in Java and `KRAFT 4.2` in YAML). **Health has three states, because there are three**: `isHealthy` is `boolean | null` and starts `null` — it started `true`, so the pill was green before anything had been probed, and stayed green for as long as an unreachable broker takes not to answer. Unknown is neutral grey with a pulsing dot (`connectionTone`), and it is what a screen reader is told too.

The router is a **data router** (`createBrowserRouter` + `RouterProvider`, a `Shell` layout route holding the `Layout` and the `Suspense` boundary, built once at module scope), not `<BrowserRouter>`. No loader is used; the reason is `useBlocker`, which only exists on a data router and is the single point where react-router lets an *internal* navigation be intercepted before it happens — `beforeunload` never sees a sidebar click.

**Typed data survives leaving the page** — `draftStore.ts` (`usePersistentState`, `readDraft`/`writeDraft`/`clearDraft`, keys under `kse:draft:`). Routes mount and unmount their page, so every `useState` on it died on navigation: the four-step Process Mining pipeline (two model calls deep), a half-written metric, unapplied connection settings. Writes are best-effort — quota, private mode and corrupt JSON all fall back to the empty form rather than throwing, and a draft past `MAX_DRAFT_BYTES` (512 kB) is dropped *and* cleared, since a stale draft read back is worse than none. Three rules keep it from becoming a nuisance: values are stored in a versioned envelope carrying the write time and **expire after `MAX_DRAFT_AGE_MS` (7 days)** — a three-week-old draft would reopen a pipeline on topics that may no longer exist — an unrecognised envelope is erased rather than guessed at, and **nothing is written until the form actually moves**, so merely visiting Process Mining no longer seeds thirteen keys for someone who typed nothing (a draft that already existed keeps being maintained, including back at the initial value: erasing there would decide for the caller, whose explicit gesture is `clearDraft`). `useDraftConflict(names)` listens on the `storage` event — fired only in *other* tabs, so no self-echo — and the page **says** that another tab is editing the same draft rather than synchronising: overwriting a form in mid-typing because something changed elsewhere is worse than the problem. Wired into `ProcessMining` (pipeline acquis only — `processMiningDraft.ts`'s `resumableStep` refuses to reopen `PROFILING`, whose request died with the page, or a live `RESULTS`, whose SSE stream did, and the page says it resumed), the `Metrics` editor modal (restored open, cleared when the modal is closed — closing *is* the cancel gesture), `Compare`, and `Config`. **`Config` never drafts a secret**: `configDraft.ts` is an allowlist (`DRAFTABLE_FIELDS`), so `truststorePassword` / `keystorePassword` / `keyPassword` / `confluentSecret` / `llmApiKey` stay out of `localStorage`, as does server-owned state (`isConnected`, `llmApiKeyConfigured`) — the server's response is the base and the draft goes on top, which is also why `savedRef` tracks the server's version and a restored form correctly reads as modified.

**Navigation is centralized** in `navigation.ts` (single source of truth): `NAV_ITEMS` grouped into **Explore** (SQL Editor, Compare, Stream Flow) / **Observe** (Metrics, Audit, Cluster) / **Analyze** (Lineage, Process Mining), plus pinned Dashboard, and `CONFIG_ITEM` (Settings) / `HELP_ITEM` (Help). `resolvePageName()` and `groupNavItems()` derive the Header breadcrumb and Sidebar sections. Add a screen here, not in each component.

**Command palette** — `CommandPalette` (⌘K / Ctrl+K, wired in `Layout`) is the single global search over quick actions, pages, Kafka topics and Flink tables, fully keyboard-driven. What an operator types there is very often an identifier rather than a page name, so any text that is not a known topic offers **`Trace "<text>" across topics`** first (`buildTraceLinkForKey`, exact-key mode): an investigation starts from anywhere, instead of navigating to an empty form and retyping the key into it.

**Design-system library** — `components/ui/` (`import { … } from '../components/ui'`), built on the `tailwind.config.js` + `index.css` tokens; prefer it for any new surface: `Button`, `Card`/`CardHeader`, `Badge`, `EmptyState`, `ErrorPanel`, `Tooltip`/`HelpTip`, `PageHeader`, `Stat`, `Field`/`Input`/`Select`/`Textarea`, `Combobox`/`TopicInput`/`NumberInput`/`PasswordInput`, `Checkbox`, `Table` (+ `Th`/`Td`/…), the `Skeleton` family, `Spinner`/`ProgressBar`, `ConfirmProvider`/`useConfirm` (async confirm dialogs), `useUnsavedGuard` (confirm before leaving an unsaved screen — `useBlocker` for internal navigation *and* `beforeunload` for reload/close, two different exits, asked through the same `useConfirm` dialog), `useVirtualRows` (row virtualization), and `cn()` (clsx + tailwind-merge). Other shared components: `Toast`/`ToastProvider`, `ErrorBanner`, `LoadingSpinner`.

**The three SVG graphs share one viewport** — `components/graph/useGraphViewport.ts`, used by
`Lineage`, `StreamFlow` and `DataModel`. They carried the same implementation copied three times:
the same `isPanning` / `lastPos` refs, the same three pointer handlers line-for-line, the same
non-passive `wheel` listener, the same keyboard step. That is exactly the shape that lets a fix
land on one page and not the other two, and it had already happened here — the move to *pointer*
events, without which a tablet cannot pan a graph at all, had to be made three times.

The split is **mechanics in the hook, policy in the pages**. The hook owns the transform state,
the gestures, the wheel zoom and the viewport measurement, and exposes `panBy` / `zoomAround` /
`zoomFromCenter` so a page's keyboard handler is a few lines. What stays in each page is what
genuinely differs: what `0` recadres (a fit for Stream Flow and Data Model, a fixed origin for
Lineage), what `Escape` deselects, and which node a table selection brings into view. Absorbing
those through three more callbacks would have made the hook harder to read than the forty lines
it replaces.

Three things are parameters rather than constants because the pages really disagree: the scale
bounds (Data Model goes to 0.1–3, the other two 0.15–4 — a hundred entities need the lower
floor), the initial transform, and whether a press on a `[data-node]` starts a pan. `viewAdjusted`
is the hook's, with `markFitted()` / `markAdjusted()` as the pair of verbs: `panBy` and
`zoomAround` raise it themselves, and a page that sets its own transform — centring on an entity —
says so, because Data Model's automatic refit on a resize must not undo a framing the operator
chose.

**`svgRef` is a callback ref, not an object ref, and that is load-bearing.** Stream Flow and Data
Model render their canvas only once a result has arrived, so an effect keyed on anything else runs
once with `ref.current` still null and never runs again — which is exactly how the `wheel` listener
stopped attaching on two of the three graphs when this hook was extracted. Each page used to work
around it with its own dependency (`[hasResult, nodes.length]`, `[zoomAround, model]`) under a
comment reading "re-attach after the SVG mounts"; sharing the code dropped the dependency and the
defect came back. A callback ref makes the mount observable, so the question no longer reaches the
caller — and pages read the element as `canvas` rather than `svgRef.current`.

Only `zoomTransform` is unit-tested; the rest is pointer plumbing over geometry jsdom does not
have, where a test would assert that mocks were called rather than that a graph pans. What covers
it instead is **`docs/screenshots/graph-gestures.mjs`**, run by CI in the screenshot job: real
pointer, wheel and keyboard events against the compiled SPA in Chromium, asserting that the pan
follows the pointer, that the zoom stays anchored under the cursor, that the keyboard reaches the
graph and that `touch-action` is neutralised. It is what found the wheel defect above.
**`Checkbox` exists for a measured reason, and is deliberately 24 × 24.** `layout-probe.mjs` counts targets under the 24 × 24 CSS px of WCAG 2.5.8, and on the Data Model page — the worst ratio after the SQL editor — **40 of the 42 undersized targets were outside the graph**, almost all native `<input type="checkbox">` at the browser's default **13 × 13**, one per topic in the selector. It was never forty problems but one control, on the six screens that carry checkboxes; the measured effect is `data-model` 42 → 9 at 390 px, `audit` 30 → 24, `topic-explorer` 14 → 11. The visible box really is larger — keeping a 16 px box and widening only the hit area is the tempting version and does not work: `padding` is not reliably applied to a replaced control, and a `transform` shrinks the real target along with the drawing, fooling the probe exactly as much as the user. It stays a native input, so semantics, keyboard and form behaviour are the browser's, and its accent comes from the `primary` token — three call sites had hardcoded `#a3adff`, which *is* `primary`.
**An explanation that only the mouse can reach is not an explanation.** `title=""` shows up on hover and nowhere else — never on keyboard focus, never on touch, with a delay and a rendering the browser owns and screen readers do not reliably announce. `Tooltip` opens on hover *and* focus, closes on `Escape`, and keeps its content mounted so `aria-describedby` always resolves; `HelpTip` is the small ⓘ trigger for controls that are not themselves inviting to hover, such as a `<select>` or a checkbox. Keep `title` for a bare affordance ("Copy"), and use the component wherever the text explains a *semantic* — what a mode compares, what an operator ignores, what a budget bounds, what an engine cannot do. The pass covered Topic Explorer, Stream Flow, Audit, the SQL editor and Metrics; what stays a `title` elsewhere only reveals a truncated or compacted value (the exact number behind `1.2K`, the absolute date behind a relative one), where a tooltip would add a tab stop per table cell and cost keyboard users more than it gives them. In `Metrics` the component is imported as `InfoTooltip`: Recharts exports a `Tooltip` of its own, and the file uses both.
**A failure that needs acting on is not a toast.** `ErrorPanel` renders a `QueryErrorInfo` (from `describeApiError`) with the three levels the SQL editor already had to itself — readable title, actionable hint, raw server text one click away — and stays on screen. A toast lasts three seconds, which suits a confirmation and not a refusal: `catch { toast('Failed to save metric') }` threw away the only useful part, the reason. Reach for the toast on success and for the panel on failure.

**Form conventions** — build every form out of these; hand-rolled `<input className="…">` blocks drift from the tokens and skip the accessibility wiring.
- `Field` owns the label ↔ control ↔ error/description `aria` plumbing and renders its child through a render prop (`{p => <Input {...p} />}`). Pass an explicit `id` when the form needs to focus the first invalid control after validation.
- **Validate every field at once**, into a `Partial<Record<field, string>>` handed to each `Field error=…`, and focus the first offender — not one message at a time in a banner at the bottom of the page.
- `NumberInput` keeps the raw string while typing and only coerces on blur. Never `parseInt(e.target.value) || fallback` on change: clearing the field snaps it to the fallback mid-typing, and `0` is falsy so a leading zero does too.
- `catalogStore` carries **what `Layout`'s existing `/api/dashboard` poll already answered**, and nothing else: the topics, the Flink tables, `internalPrefix`, and `bootstrapServers`. That last one was read by the Metrics page through a `GET /api/config` posted at mount for that single field, under a response shape declared by hand at the call site (`{ bootstrapServers: string }`) — a request more, and a shape `check-api-types.py` cannot see, which is the pattern that killed the Compare page. It comes off `DashboardResponse`, which is `@java`-verified and already carried it. The rule is the one written beside `internalPrefix`: a value belongs here when it is *the same response* that brings it, and a second carrier is how two answers to one question begin.
- `TopicInput` suggests topic (or Flink table) names from `catalogStore`, which `Layout` fills from its existing `/api/dashboard` poll — no extra request. Free text stays valid: a topic can exist before the 30s cache shows it. **Opening the list deliberately — the chevron, or ArrowDown from the field — shows every option, unfiltered** (`browseAll` in `Combobox`); typing goes straight back to filtering. Suggestions are filtered by the field's own value, so a value already chosen filtered the list down to one entry identical to itself, which the "a suggestion equal to what you typed teaches nothing" rule then dropped: the list was empty from the moment anything was selected and the chevron did nothing at all. In the metric editor, which pre-fills the topic with `topics[0]`, that removed the ability to change the topic from the list outright — the `<select>` it replaced could always be reopened.
- `PasswordInput` adds a reveal toggle and `autoComplete="new-password"`. Secrets typed blind fail at connection time with nothing to diagnose.
- Wrap in a real `<form onSubmit>` so Enter submits from any field. `Button` defaults to `type="button"` for that reason — submit buttons declare `type="submit"` explicitly.

**Routes / pages** (`pages/`):
- `Dashboard` (`/`) — topic list with filtering. **The refresh cadence is a control, and the page dates what it shows** (`pages/dashboardRefresh.ts`, pure + tested): the poll was a module constant — 5 s, not adjustable, not switchable off, and never stated — yet every turn calls `/api/dashboard`, which lists the topics, their sizes and their last-message timestamps. The reads are cached 30 s server-side, so six turns in seven re-read the same entry and the seventh pays the broker round trips; nobody watching a pipeline start could speed that up, and nobody keeping the page on a second screen could slow it down. Four rules hold it. **The default is today's behaviour** (`DEFAULT_REFRESH_CHOICE` = the 5 s that were hardcoded): making a cadence adjustable must not change what an existing install does. **`Off` is a value of the same selector**, like the activity column's, and for the same reason — the poll costs broker round trips, so it has to be switchable from the screen and not only from a configuration file — but **returning to the tab still reads once**, polling off included: what `Off` asks is that the broker not be queried in a loop behind the operator, not that they be shown hour-old numbers at the moment they come back to look. **The activity column follows the same setting with its own floor** (`activityIntervalMs`, `ACTIVITY_FLOOR_MS` = the 30 s server cache): the choice *slows* it and never speeds it up — a 2 s dashboard must not hammer a 30 s cache — and `Off` stops both, one switch, or the page would go on polling after being told to stop. And **the page says when it read and what it will do next** (`describeRefreshStatus`): `fetchedAt` existed and only fed the relative last-message times, so nothing stated the age of the figures. That was survivable while the cadence was 5 s for everyone and stops being so the moment it can be five minutes or nothing — a number nobody can date is a number nobody should act on, the same rule as the Prometheus gauges. Both halves together: the age alone reads as a breakdown, the cadence alone doesn't say where in the interval you are. An **activity sparkline per row** sits beside it (`components/dashboard/Sparkline.tsx`, pure logic + tests in `pages/topicActivity.ts`). The table said how many messages a topic holds and when the last one arrived, which is a level and an instant — neither says whether the topic is *working*, and "1 200 messages, 3 min ago" reads the same on a topic doing forty a minute and on one that received a burst yesterday and stopped. Four rules keep the column from claiming more than it measured. It reads **only the rows on screen** (`pagedTopics`), on its own 30 s cadence rather than the dashboard's 5 s poll — asking for a nine-hundred-topic cluster would spend an offset sweep nobody is looking at, and re-asking every 5 s would only re-read the same cache entry six times. The **scale is per row** (`sparkline`), since a scale shared across the table would flatten every topic that is not the busiest of the cluster — so the peak is **written beside the curve** (`describeRate`, `620/h`), not only in the tooltip: an explanation only the mouse can reach is the exact defect `title=""` was replaced for elsewhere, and two curves of equal height can be 40/h and 40 000/h. Hovering a bucket replaces that number with the bucket's own value and marks it on the curve, rather than opening a twenty-sixth tooltip. **The curve is a button and the click leads to the messages** (`bucketLink`): seeing a spike and reading what was in it are the same question asked twice, and the second half was being done by hand. It opens the topic's explorer with the search *primed* at that bucket's instant — primed and not run, because the topic search has no "everything" criterion by design (KEY + `EXISTS` was deliberately removed from that UI for returning everything scanned), so the link carries what it knows and the operator says what they are looking for. It travels through the **URL** (`?start=TIMESTAMP&at=…`, read back by `seedFromQuery`) rather than through the explorer's `localStorage` draft: that is the convention of every other screen here, it makes the link shareable, and it does not silently overwrite an unrun criterion on another topic. A button rather than a bare SVG because the affordance has to exist for the keyboard too — `Enter` opens the **peak** bucket, which is what the accessible name already names. That adds one tab stop per row, which this codebase refuses for a *tooltip* and accepts here for the same reason the row's `Explore` link is one: there is an action behind it. **Beside the peak sits the current regime, when it departs from the topic's own** (`detectTrend`, `▲ 2.4×`): the shape answers that question badly at the size of a cell and the peak does not answer it at all — it describes the busiest moment of the window, which may be twenty hours old. The reference is the window's **median**, not its mean, which the very burst one is trying to situate would pull; and the ratio reads without knowing the topic, where `620/h` does not. Same discipline as the badge beside it: at least six buckets with four non-empty (a median needs matter), a non-zero median (a ratio against zero is a division dressed up as information), and a departure of at least a factor two — below that it is the ordinary noise of a live topic, and an indicator that lights up always is one that stops being read. An **empty** last bucket produces nothing here: that is `detectSilence`'s case, which says it better and has its own threshold. **A topic that produced and stopped says so** (`detectSilence`, an amber `silent 6 h+` in place of the peak): it is the one thing a curve of this size does not show — a series fallen to zero and a low series look alike — and `Last Message` only gives the instant, not the fact that there was a regime before it. Three guards keep the badge from being noise (a prior regime of at least two non-empty buckets, a silence of at least two buckets *and* 15 % of the window, and a window that is not entirely empty — that last one is not a topic that went quiet, it is a topic nobody saw produce), and what it states is a dated fact rather than a verdict: `silent 4 h+` is true of a topic that sleeps at night, and whether that is a problem is the operator's to know. The duration is a **floor** — the last message sits somewhere inside its bucket — which is what the `+` carries. **Nothing is drawn where nothing was measured**: the stretch retention emptied is greyed rather than traced at zero, a series short of a partition is dashed, a topic that could not be read says `not measured` instead of showing a flat line, and a failed read replaces the curves with its reason instead of leaving them flat — a flat line is a claim that the topic is quiet. And the **window is a control** (`Last hour` / `Last 24 h` / `Last 7 days` / `Off`, kept in `kse:dashboard-activity`): `Off` is one of its values because the column costs broker round trips, so it must be switchable from the screen and not only from a configuration file. Each window carries its own resolution, and the week's is **56 points of 3 h, not 28 of 6 h**: over seven days the question is almost always whether the shape repeats, and a day reduced to four points shows no cycle at all — the night and the morning fall in the same one.
