The cost is the server's and is bounded there (57 boundaries per partition instead of 29, all issued in parallel, all counted against `explorer.activity-max-lookups`). **The vertical scale is a second control** (`Linear` / `Log`, `kse:dashboard-activity-scale`, `ActivityScale`): a burst a hundred times the ordinary regime crushes everything else onto the baseline, and what happens the rest of the time is usually what one is trying to read. It stays an option rather than the default because changing the scale changes what the image asserts — under `log` two heights are no longer in the ratio of their values — which is also why the column header names it when it is on: an undeclared scale is precisely what makes a chart mislead. `log1p` rather than `log`, so an empty bucket sits exactly on the baseline instead of running to minus infinity or needing an arbitrary offset, and the peak is the top of the box under either scale. It is a **reading** control and touches one thing: the shape. It changes nothing that is asked of the server, and nothing the accessible name states, which is raw counts either way. The response is held with **the question it answers** (`activityState.key` = window + topics), so changing page or window never leaves the previous series under the new header for the length of a round trip. The column is deliberately **not sortable**: it is measured for the displayed rows only, so sorting the cluster on it would sort on what was never read.
- `QueryController` — `/api/query/**`. A path-less `@PostMapping` duplicating `/run-sync` was removed: nothing here ever posted to it, so it was a second public entry point to the query engine that no test exercised — two paths to one behaviour is how they drift. `ddl-preview` reports a failure through `SqlErrorClassifier.explain()`, **not** `e.getMessage()`: that is null for a `NullPointerException` and `Map.of` rejects a null value, so the handler's own error path answered 500 in exactly the case where the caller most needs a reason. Its `@RequestParam` is named explicitly so the mapping resolves without `-parameters` on the compiler command line (the offline harness compiles with plain `javac`). `init` **reports why it came back empty** (`kafkaError` / `flinkError` on `QueryInitResponse`) instead of ending both of its probes in an empty catch: an unreachable broker, a wrong bootstrap address, an authorisation failure and a Flink runtime still starting up all rendered as the same "Engine offline · 0 tables · 0 topics". The probes stay independent — one side failing must not empty the other — and a broker that answers `pingDetail()` but refuses the metadata call keeps `health: true` with the reason beside it, since "offline" would send an operator to check a connection that works. `cancel` **says what it achieved** (`{cancelled, outcome}` from `FlinkSqlService.CancelOutcome`) rather than returning `void` behind a 200: a `KAFKA_DIRECT` scan has no Flink job by construction, so "nothing to cancel" is the common case and the UI must not report it as a cancellation — it says "Request aborted — the server finishes its in-flight read" instead. Still 200 either way, since nothing to cancel is a legitimate outcome of a well-formed request. `QueryControllerTest` pins the three endpoints with standalone MockMvc.
- `QueryWorkbench` (`/query`, nav "SQL Editor") — Monaco SQL editor; saved queries + history in `localStorage` (`kse:saved-queries`, `kse:query-history`). **Every failure lands in one error panel**: `pages/queryError.ts` (`describeQueryError` / `describeApiError`, pure and unit-tested) turns a raw engine message into a readable title, an actionable hint and — when the message cites one — a line/column, which drives both a "jump to line" button and a native Monaco error marker; the raw text is always kept behind "Show raw error". The `panelError` state carries failures that no `QueryResult` holds (execution-mode rejection, `POST /api/query/validate`, transport failure) through that same panel, and is cleared as soon as the SQL is edited so a stale marker never points at a moved position. `QueryResult.warnings` is rendered above the grid — the direct engine reports the WHERE predicates it could not apply, and dropping them presented an unfiltered scan as a filtered one. Tabs persist to `localStorage` (`kse:tabs`, restored once at first render, **written on a 400 ms debounce** — the effect ran on every keystroke, so each character cost a `JSON.stringify` of every tab and a synchronous storage write; a `?sql=` opens an *extra* tab rather than clobbering the first), which is why there is no longer a `beforeunload` guard. **The `?sql=` is then stripped from the URL**: since the tabs are persisted and the parameter was not, every reload re-appended the same tab, without bound. Closing a tab that holds SQL asks first — that text exists nowhere else afterwards — and `nextActiveTabId` picks the successor *outside* the `setTabs` updater, which used to call `setActiveTabId` from within it. The row cap is a real control wired to `maxRows` — the "limit reached" badge compares against the limit *that query* ran with, not a frontend constant that silently drifted from `explorer.default-max-rows`. Autocomplete and hover cover **Kafka topics as well as Flink tables** — they read `schema.tables` only, while the sidebar lists topics, clicking one writes `SELECT * FROM demo_orders_1_received`, and the backend auto-registers a topic on a plain SELECT, so typing that name got no help at all; topics are offered under their table name, labelled and sorted behind the registered tables. Column autocomplete is scoped to the tables the query actually cites (`pages/sqlScope.ts`, pure and unit-tested: comments and string literals stripped, `TABLE(TUMBLE(TABLE t, …))` understood, topic↔table naming normalized), and their schemas are fetched on a 500 ms debounce, at most once per name per catalog refresh. **What Run sends**: a non-empty editor selection when there is one, otherwise **the statement the cursor is in** (`splitStatements` / `statementIndexAt`, which walk strings, quoted identifiers and both comment forms so a `;` inside any of them separates nothing, and pick the statement *just finished* when the cursor follows its semicolon — as DataGrip and DBeaver do). Run used to send the whole tab whatever the cursor was in while the backend classified on the first word, so selecting by hand was the only way to run one query among several. `offsetLocation()` maps the engine's line/column back into document coordinates in both cases; without it the Monaco marker would point at the top of the file. The toolbar states `Statement 2/3` before you press, and **`Run all`** (⌘⇧↵) executes them in order, stopping at the first failure and naming it — creating a table then reading it is what auto-registration encourages, and it had become two gestures.
  **Running several statements keeps what each one gave, not only the last.** `planRun` decides what a gesture sends — the selection when there is one, the cursor's statement otherwise, every statement for `Run all` — and a **selection spanning several statements now runs them one after another** instead of going to the planner as one query it rejects on the `;`; a selected *fragment* still goes verbatim, since trying a sub-expression has to keep working, and a tab holding only blanks or comments answers `Nothing to run` rather than asking the engine to say what was already known. Each statement of a batch keeps a `StatementRun` (kind, status, duration, rows, engine, result), listed in a strip above the grid: selecting one brings its rows back **with the row cap it ran under**, since the "limit reached" badge must not judge an old result by how the selector is set now. `runAllStatements` used to call a loop that begins with `setResults(null)`, so five statements left one grid and no trace of the other four — no rows, no duration, no engine, no proof they had run. Retention is bounded and **stated**: `forgetOldestResults` releases the oldest rows past `MAX_RETAINED_BATCH_ROWS` (10 000 across the batch, never the entry on screen) and the released ones render as "4 000 rows, no longer held" with a rerun button — an empty grid would read as zero rows, which is the opposite answer. Statements the batch never reached are `skipped`, distinct from a run that returned nothing. Three things that could go wrong while a batch ran are closed: `executeStatement` returns an outcome (`ok` / `failed` / `cancelled`) rather than a boolean, so a **Stop is no longer reported in red as a failure**; `batchRef` refuses a `⌘↵` landing *between* two statements, where `executingRef` is false and a keystroke started a query concurrent with the loop; and **Stop stops the batch**, through a flag the loop re-reads after each statement — aborting the in-flight request did nothing in that same gap, and the button was not even rendered there. **The mode gates none of this**: `Statement 2/3` and `Run all` were shown in Read mode only, so a tab of three `INSERT INTO` — the shape Flink Job mode exists for — submitted one job with nothing saying so. And `isResultStale` now consults the tab's statements: comparing what ran against the whole tab made every multi-statement result permanently "stale", and a warning that is always on stops being a warning. A **`Link`** button copies a replayable `?sql=` URL: the editor accepted one without ever producing one, unlike Stream Flow and the Topic Explorer. `readSqlParam` does the **single** decode `URLSearchParams` already performs — the caller decoded a second time, which throws `URIError` on any `%`, so a shared `LIKE '%foo%'` took the whole page down from inside a `useState` initializer — "Run statement" without saying which one is the worrying half of the feature. The Window Assistant (`pages/windowSql.ts`, pure and unit-tested) **folds to a rail** (its state in `kse:query-layout`; it used to cost the editor 288 px permanently for an occasional tool) and emits genuinely different SQL per window type (the dropdown used to produce `TUMBLE` whatever the choice), pre-fills the time column from the loaded schema instead of hardcoding `event_time`, states its caveats (SESSION needs a `PARTITION BY`; HOP/SESSION are approximated if the query falls back), and **replaces the whole editor content** rather than inserting at the cursor: what it emits is a complete query, not a fragment, so an insertion almost always left two statements stuck together to untangle by hand. The replacement goes through Monaco's edit API over the full model range rather than through `updateSql` — writing the value from React calls `model.setValue()`, which clears the undo stack, so the overwritten query would be gone for good; through `executeEdits` it is one ⌘Z away, and the toast says so. A tab that already holds SQL asks first (`useConfirm`, as everywhere else): the undo rescues the loss but is only discovered afterwards, and one click must not erase a query being written without saying so — an empty tab has nothing to lose and asks nothing. CSV export goes through `pages/resultExport.ts` (RFC 4180): the old `JSON.stringify` per cell left a multi-key object's comma unquoted, shifting every following column — and it now serialises **what is displayed**, `sortedRows`, where it used to take `results.rows` and hand back a file in a different order from the screen.
  **The sidebar's shortcut follows the execution mode** (`sidebarSqlFor` / `sidebarActionLabel`, pure and unit-tested): Read mode poses `SELECT * FROM <table> LIMIT <maxRows>`, Flink Job mode an `INSERT INTO <table>_out SELECT * FROM <table>`. It always wrote the SELECT, which the mode guard then refuses in Job mode ("Flink Job mode only accepts INSERT INTO") — so clicking a topic there had exactly one possible outcome, an error panel. **The target is taken from the catalogue when it holds one** (`pickSinkTable`, preferring a table named after the source — a derived sink almost always is — then the first non-`internal` one, the source itself excluded): an invented name can only fail, where a registered table *resolves*. That is all it promises, so the comment says the columns still have to accept the projection rather than implying the statement typechecks. With no candidate it falls back to `<source>_out`, and the comment then says placeholder instead of claiming the table exists — announcing "an existing table" above an invented name would be the exact opposite of the point. Either way the target is **posed selected** (`sinkNameRange`, anchored at a line start so the word INSERT inside those comments cannot be mistaken for the statement), so the first keystroke replaces it. No `LIMIT` on that branch — nothing bounds a streaming job. **The columns are listed rather than `SELECT *`** (`insertableColumns`): `DdlGeneratorService` ends every table it generates with `proc_time AS PROCTIME()`, a computed column that comes out of a `SELECT *` but that no sink accepts, so an INSERT between two generated tables failed on an arity mismatch — an error that does not name its cause. The exclusion is by name for want of anything better: `/api/query/schema/{table}` returns name → data type, and a type does not say whether a column is computed (Flink's `isPersisted()` would, but it is not exposed); `event_time` stays, `METADATA FROM 'timestamp'` being persistable. The schema is fetched on the click when missing, and a table Flink does not know yet returns an empty one — the SQL then falls back to `SELECT *` and says in a comment what that risks. The button's accessible name follows too; it announced "SELECT from …" whatever the mode, which is the only name a screen reader gets.
  **Nothing on this page silently replaces the tab you are writing in** (`openSql`): the sidebar's "SELECT from this table", every Kafka topic in the sidebar, every history entry and the DDL preview's "Insert in editor" all called `updateSql()`, which overwrites the *whole active tab* — clicking a topic to see its shape erased the query in progress, with no confirmation and no undo. That is exactly the defect the Window Assistant was fixed for, left in four other places. One rule now: an empty tab is filled, a tab with content is untouched and the SQL opens in a new one, which is what loading a saved query already did.
  **Monaco ships no SQL formatter**, so the "Format" button — which ran `editor.action.formatDocument` — had never reformatted anything; with no provider registered the action only prints a discreet "There is no formatter for 'sql' files installed" inside the editor. `pages/queryWorkbench.ts` supplies `formatSql`, registered as a `DocumentFormattingEditProvider` **in `monaco-setup.ts`** — the provider is global to the Monaco instance, so registering it from a page effect (disposed on unmount) left the app's three other SQL editors, two in `Metrics` and one in `TopicExplorer`, without one. It moves string literals, quoted identifiers and comments but never rewrites them (`WHERE label = 'group by'` is the test that pins it), and upper-cases function names from a **closed** list: an unknown one is most likely a UDF, and Flink does not promise case-insensitive resolution for those, so recasing `XmlExtract` would break the query being tidied.
  `pages/queryWorkbench.ts` also holds the rest of the page's pure logic, all unit-tested: `sortRows` (one key extracted per row instead of a `localeCompare` collator call per comparison — ~60 000 of them for 5 000 rows — comparing numbers as numbers, stable, absent values last **in both directions**), `cellText` (a SQL `NULL` and a zero-length string rendered identically, which is the distinction that says whether a LEFT JOIN found its row), `isResultStale` (the grid kept the previous run's rows while the next query was typed, silently), `readLayout` (the split and sidebar widths, persisted under `kse:query-layout` and clamped on read) and `writeStored`/`readStored`/`removeStored` — three of the four `localStorage` writes on this page were unguarded, so a full quota threw inside a click handler.
  `⌘↵` is registered from `onMount`, using the Monaco API that callback receives: registered from an effect keyed on `[monaco]` it bailed out whenever `useMonaco()` resolved before the editor mounted — which, Monaco being bundled locally, is the common order — and never re-ran, so the advertised shortcut was simply absent. A second `⌘↵` while a query runs is refused through a synchronous ref: the Run button disables itself but the keybinding bypassed it, and the second run overwrote the abort controller and query id, leaving Stop unable to cancel the first.
  **A new tab opens empty**, and the results panel offers starter queries built from the catalogue that actually loaded (`starterQueries`). The first tab used to be seeded with a hardcoded query that could not run: `WINDOW TUMBLING (SIZE 5 MINUTES) … EMIT CHANGES` is ksqlDB syntax rather than Flink SQL, and `orders_stream` exists in no dataset here — so the most natural first gesture, open the editor and press Run, failed on a planner error. Suggestions derived from the catalogue cannot name an absent table, skip the `internal.*` topics the app writes to itself, and are simply absent when the catalogue is empty. **They follow the execution mode** (`starterJobQueries`): they were gated on Read mode, so Job mode — where the expected shape, an `INSERT INTO` and nothing else, is the least obvious — offered no starting point at all. The Job starter is the *same* SQL the sidebar poses, target selected included, so one gesture is not written two ways depending on where it is triggered; `StarterQuery.preview` carries the one-line form, since the card truncates and the SQL opens with its comments.
  **Clicking a cell opens the row beside the grid**, not a blind copy: the grid forces one line per row once virtualised, so a long value was reachable only through its `title` on hover and a nested document stayed a run of text. `RowDetail` shows every column of the selected row with `detailValue` indenting what is structured — including JSON that arrived **as a string**, which is what the direct engine returns for a sub-document — and carries the copy, per value. It closes on `Escape`, steps between rows, and closes itself when a sort reorders the grid, since the index it holds would then point at a different row.
  **A history entry says what the query did** — duration, rows, engine, outcome (`HistoryEntry` / `pushHistory` / `describeHistoryEntry`) — and **failures are recorded**, since the query one wants to reopen is very often the one that failed. Every result field is optional, so an entry written by an earlier version reads back and shows less; a cancelled run is not recorded at all.
  The page's parts live in `components/query/` (`SchemaBrowser`, `ResultsGrid`, `RowDetail`, `WindowAssistant`, `DdlPreviewModal`) — it was a single 2 168-line component, and the split came *after* `QueryWorkbench.test.tsx` existed, not before. `WindowAssistant` owns its own form state and stays mounted when folded, so folding does not reset it.
  See `SQL-EDITOR-AUDIT.md` for the full review, including what was deliberately left open.
- `TopicExplorer` (`/topic/:name`) — message sampling, schema preview and **server-side search** over the whole topic (`components/topic/TopicSearchPanel.tsx`): text / regex / field / header / key modes (plus a "search headers too" switch that widens a text or regex pass to every header value, and a "only this key's partition" switch on an exact key search), the field list fed by the inferred schema, a time range, and a status strip stating hits, records scanned and why the pass stopped. Pure logic (critère, corps de requête, couverture, surlignage, URL) lives in `components/topic/topicSearch.ts` and is unit-tested; the panel only renders what it decides.
  **Which end the scan enters by is a choice, and the default is the newest** (`ScanDirection`, `buildSearchBody`): `NEWEST` sends `from: LAST_N`, so the server walks back from the end and a time window *raises that floor* instead of replacing it. The panel used to send `EARLIEST` always (`TIMESTAMP` with a window), which spent the whole 20 000-record budget on the oldest history of a topic with millions — a record from ten minutes ago was reported as "scan budget reached, 0 match", and the page's own Earliest/Latest toggle (which only drives sampling) made that look deliberate. Because a cursor points *forward*, past the newest record read, "continue scanning" cannot reach older records: `nextScanAction` therefore offers `RESUME` (cursor) only while the pass has not reached the end of its range, and `DEEPEN` — the same `LAST_N` read again with a doubled `maxScan`, up to the server's 1 000 000 cap, results replaced — once it has. For the same reason `describeCoverage` never says "whole range scanned" on a `NEWEST` pass: it says *newest N records scanned*, since everything before the window read was never opened.
  **The coverage is that of every pass together** (`SearchCoverage` / `coverageOf`): a response describes only its own pass, so showing `response.scanned` after three resumes claimed 20 000 records where 60 000 had been read. A resume adds up (it reads new ground), a fresh run or a deepening replaces (it re-reads the same). **A pass is cancellable** (`AbortController` + a generation counter, like a trace): what comes back from a superseded or abandoned pass is dropped, and the panel says the pass was abandoned rather than letting the displayed results pass for its outcome.
  Messages carry their coordinates (partition, offset, timestamp, key, headers), and **a hit shows why it is one, whatever the mode**: `highlightFor` marks the literal of a text search, the pattern of a regex one (`splitOnRegex`, which returns the text unmarked on an invalid pattern and cannot loop on a zero-width match) and the compared value of a field / header / key search — but nothing at all for `NEQ` / `GT` / `EXISTS`, where marking the value would designate precisely what is *not* there. Highlighting describes the run that is displayed (`ranCriteria`), never the form as it is being edited. Kafka **headers are rendered inline** and expand on their own when the match could have been decided there (`revealsHeaders`), with the compared header named (`highlightedHeader`): they used to live in a `title` tooltip, so a HEADER search returned ten cards whose evidence was reachable only with the mouse, one at a time. The read-mode toggle drives which end of the topic is sampled, not only the generated DDL. **The unrun criterion survives leaving the page** (`readCriteriaDraft` / `saveCriteriaDraft`): the URL carries an *executed* search, so a half-typed criterion abandoned to go check a field name elsewhere died with the page. One slot, stamped with the topic it belongs to — a key per topic would grow `localStorage` without bound for a case that is always "I am coming back to the topic I just left" — and a criterion from another topic is not restored. A search carried by the URL always wins. **A URL can also *prime* the form without running it** (`seedFromQuery`): the dashboard's sparkline links here with a start instant and no criterion, which is not executable — `criteriaFromQuery` rightly answers `null` — and answering `null` while posing nothing would land the operator on an empty form, losing the only thing the link carried. The two functions are exclusive by construction (`canRun` decides which one answers), and a *present but unknown* `mode` stays refused by both rather than being reinterpreted as a text search. A search **round-trips through the URL** (`?mode=&q=&field=&op=&value=&case=&keys=&headers=&keyPartitioning=&since=&dir=&parts=&scan=`, `criteriaFromQuery` / `buildSearchQuery`): it is applied and run on open — which is how a Stream Flow hop lands on its messages instead of having the criterion retyped into a form that has different fields — and rewritten after each run so a search can be shared and replayed, with `Link` copying it and `Clear` stripping it (`Clear` keeps the criterion in the form: erasing the results should not erase the question). The page guards against reading back the query string it wrote itself, which would rerun the search that produced it, forever. The last eight searches per topic are kept in `localStorage` (`kse:topic-search-history`, de-duplicated on the criterion, entries from an older shape completed against `emptyCriteria` rather than replayed with holes), and the hits export to CSV or to JSON — the JSON carrying the criterion, the coverage and the warnings, since an export pasted into a ticket has to say what it covered.
  **Where the scan starts is a choice too** (`StartMode`, `switchStart`): a relative window, an absolute instant (`fromTimestamp`) or an offset (`fromOffset`) — the last two were served by the API and reachable from nothing, while an incident question almost always takes the form "from 14:02" or "from offset 128000", the latter usually arriving from a lag alert that already gives the number. An instant behaves exactly like the relative window it replaces (a floor under `LAST_N`, a starting point under `TIMESTAMP`); an offset is a forward read, so it overrides the direction, which then disappears from the form rather than sitting there being ignored. A future instant is refused: the broker returns no offset for it, so the floor would silently vanish and the scan would read the newest records while claiming to start from that date. `describeCoverage` never says "whole topic" after a chosen start — everything before it was never opened — and `nextScanAction` offers no deepening there, since a wider budget cannot move a fixed starting point.
  **The hit cap is a control** (`HIT_CAPS`, the API's `maxHits`, clamped to 1 000 server-side) and `raiseHitCapAction` is what a `MAX_HITS` stop offers. Resuming reads on *past* the records already scanned, so the matches skipped beyond the cap are out of reach for good — "12 of 340" with a "continue" button was an invitation to a scan that would never show them. Rereading with a higher cap is the only gesture that does, and it replaces the results.
  **A truncated record can be read whole** (`GET /api/topic/{name}/record?partition=&offset=` → `TopicSearchService.readRecord`): a hit is cut to `search-max-value-chars` so that a hundred of them stay a small response, which left the `truncated` badge pointing at a remainder nothing could fetch. Reading one record on purpose is the opposite need, so it has its own budget (`explorer.record-max-value-chars`, 1 000 000) — and still reports `truncated` and the original size at that boundary. The endpoint answers 404 when the offset holds no record: out of range, or compacted away, which a caller must be able to tell from a failure.
  **The scan can be narrowed and deepened from the screen**: a partition chip row (the API's `partitions`, which the server honours *over* key partitioning and without warning — hence `keyPartitioningApplies`, which stops offering the key switch once a partition is picked, and `describePartitionScope`, which states the restriction in the coverage strip, or a "0 match" on one partition of twenty-four would read like a "0 match" on the topic), and a scan budget (`SCAN_BUDGETS`, the API's `maxScan`, which the server clamps to a million). `describeScanShare` situates the pass against the topic's estimated size — 20 000 records read means something different on a topic of 30 000 and on one of two million. When nothing matched, `suggestWidenings` turns the advice into at most four one-click reruns (match anywhere in the field, search the raw payload, search headers, ignore case, every partition, drop the window, a bigger budget), each changing one thing and stating it.
  **A message has its own link** (`?record=p:offset`, `recordFromQuery` / `withRecord` / `buildRecordLink`): the URL described a search, never a record, so pointing a colleague at *this* message meant describing how to find it again. The parameter is independent of the criterion — a URL carrying only it runs no search, one carrying both shows both, and a search rewriting the URL keeps it — and the record is read through the same coordinate endpoint, so a compacted or aged-out offset answers with the server's own reason rather than an empty panel.
  **The working loop**: a **Follow** toggle repeats the cursor resume every `FOLLOW_INTERVAL_MS` (5 s) — resuming *is* a tail, since the cursor points past the last record read, so this only automates a click an operator was making by hand; it stops on an error, a cancel or a clear, and a resumed pass no longer writes history (a follow would rewrite it every five seconds). The value field in FIELD mode becomes a `Combobox` fed by `valuesAtPath` over the samples already loaded — no extra request, and free text stays valid — because nobody knows from memory whether the status reads `SHIPPED`, `shipped` or `Shipped`, which is the most banal cause of a zero. Searches **pin** (`kse:topic-search-pinned`, `togglePinned`) beside the history that scrolls past. Keyboard: `/` focuses the search field, `j` / `k` walk the hits in table view (through `nextSelectedRank`, over the *displayed* list so a filtered-out record is never selected), `Escape` deselects, all of it ignored while a field has the focus (`isTypingTarget`); `announceResult` fills a visually hidden `role="status"`, since the disappearance of "Searching…" tells a screen-reader user nothing.
  **The panel is a real `<form>`**: Enter submits from any field, `validateCriteria` checks every field at once into a `Partial<Record<field, string>>` rendered next to each control, and the first offender is focused through the stable ids `FIELD_IDS`. The Search button is no longer disabled on an incomplete criterion — it was grey and silent about what it wanted. The regex is compiled client-side, a numeric operator refuses a non-numeric value (the server compares numbers and would silently never match), `operatorsFor` drops `EXISTS` from a key search (it answers true without comparing, so it would return every record scanned), `switchMode` carries the typed text into the input the new mode actually uses, and a failed search lands in an `ErrorPanel` through `describeApiError` instead of a small red line.
  **Hits read as cards or as a table** (`kse:topic-view`), **and the table is the default**: one arrives here from the Dashboard, which is to say without having chosen anything, to see what is in a topic — a question a table answers at a glance (coordinates, key, preview, one line per record) where cards unroll a JSON tree per message and fit three on a screen. Cards remain the right tool for reading *one* payload whole, which is the next question, and selecting a row already answers it. The stored preference wins either way, which is why `readViewMode` compares against `'cards'` and not `'table'`: built the other way it would have shown the table to whoever had explicitly chosen cards, which is not changing a default but ignoring a choice — and an unreadable stored value falls back to the default, the same rule as `readActivityChoice`. The table is one fixed-height line per record (coordinates plus a `previewOf` payload) with the detail on selection, windowed through `useVirtualRows` — a hundred hits, each rendering a recursive JSON tree, both scanned badly and cost a lot. Columns **sort** and a text box **filters what is already fetched** (`rankHits` / `filterHits` / `sortHits`) without a server pass; a hit's `rank` is its order in the scan, so it survives both, and the selected row is tracked by rank rather than by index. `analyzeHits` / `describeHitInsight` read the hits above the list — the busiest partition, the time span, the keys seen more than once — because forty rows do not show by themselves that thirty-eight are on one partition. `MessageCard` is `React.memo`-ed and its parse/format memoized, with every prop the page passes it memoized too: the criterion lives in the page, so each keystroke in the form re-rendered the whole list and re-parsed every payload.
  **Highlighting reaches the structured view**, not only the raw one: `highlightedPath` gives the compared dot path when it designates a single node (a path through an array, a predicate or a recursive descent gives `null` — marking the wrong node is worse than marking nothing), `JsonNode` / `XmlViewer` ring that node's key and mark its value, and a text or regex search marks every leaf instead.
  **The scan options fold away** (`kse:topic-search-advanced`, `readAdvancedOpen` / `describeAdvanced`): direction, budget and partitions each earn their place and together they made a bar the eye stopped scanning. Folded, whatever departs from the default is still announced as a chip — an active option nobody can see is exactly what must not happen.
  **Warnings accumulate across passes** (`mergeWarnings`, de-duplicated, replaced by a fresh run or a deepening like the coverage): showing only the last response's warnings erased, on a resume, a warning that still described part of what was on screen. It refuses anything the panel's own `Search` button would refuse (unknown mode, no value, no target), snaps a free time window onto an offered scope, and a field path absent from the inferred schema is **added to the select** rather than leaving "Select a field…" above a search that uses it. Each message with a record key carries a **`route` action** back the other way (`buildTraceLinkForKey`): it opens Stream Flow on that key in exact-key mode — the key is known exactly, so an entire-value comparison on one partition beats a substring search that would also match messages merely mentioning it
  **An `Activity` tab answers "what does this topic's production look like?"** (`components/topic/TopicActivityPanel.tsx`, the same measurement the dashboard's column carries, from the same `GET /api/dashboard/activity`). The page knew what is in the topic, who reads it and how far behind — not the shape of what it produces, which is the first thing anyone looks at on an incident. Here there is room for what a cell cannot hold: a time axis (`axisTicks`), a hover that names the bucket and its count in words, and a **click that primes the search at that instant** — seeing a spike and reading what was in it becomes one gesture instead of two screens, where the dashboard's curve has to cross a page boundary to do the same. Mounted only when the tab is opened, the same rule as the consumers panel and for the same reason: the measurement costs broker round trips and nobody arriving to read messages should pay for it unasked. The chart is one `<svg>` stretched horizontally (`preserveAspectRatio="none"` with `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"`, so the time axis fills the width without thickening the line) — which is also why nothing in it is drawn with a circle, a circle becoming an ellipse, and why the hover marker is a plain line. The response is held with the question it answers (`key` = topic + window), so switching window never leaves the previous graph under the new label, and "loading" is that same comparison rather than a state posted from an effect.
  **A `Consumers` tab answers "why is this piling up?"** (`components/topic/TopicConsumersPanel.tsx`, pure logic + tests in `topicConsumers.ts`). It is mounted only when opened — reading committed offsets costs several coordinator round trips, and nobody arriving to read messages should pay them. `healthOf` grades each group rather than showing a bare number, because the number alone misleads in three directions: `STALLED` is records waiting with **no member assigned to this topic** (nothing will drain them, whatever the count), `PARTIAL` is a group that has never committed on some partitions (the total does not count what it ignores), and `AHEAD` is a committed offset past the end of the log. Expanding a group shows the per-partition detail — where an imbalance is actually visible — with `never committed` and `—` where the API returns `null`, never a zero, and the server's **per-group reason** where a group could not be read: it was carried in the payload and displayed nowhere, so "Unreadable" was a verdict with no diagnosis. Unknown membership renders as `—`, not `0/0`. **A per-group "how long has it been waiting?" button** measures the backlog in time on demand (`GET /api/topic/{name}/time-lag?group=`): on a button and not at load, because that read opens a consumer and reads a record per lagging partition where everything else on the panel reads metadata. `describeDelay` says which of the three answers it is — measured, partially measured (the value is then a **floor**, not a maximum, and the sentence says "at least"), or not measurable — and a partition with no measurement renders `not measured`, never a zero. `describeScope` states what the read covered, so an empty list cannot pass for "nobody consumes this topic" — and a read that *failed* says so rather than borrowing either sentence
- `Compare` (`/compare`) — side-by-side topic comparison
- `StreamFlow` (`/stream-flow`) — message tracing across topics. A trace is a bounded scan and the page says so: a coverage line (`describeCoverage`) states what was read, the backend `warnings` are listed as they are, and the "no flow found" panel repeats the coverage rather than a bare "not found". The **evidence table** under the graph is the checkable part — one row per topic with hit count, first sighting, Δ from the previous hop, partition/offset and a payload preview, the topic name linking to its explorer — and selecting a row highlights the node (and vice versa). The run is **cancellable** (`AbortController`; a whole-cluster trace can legitimately take a minute), the scan window is a real choice ("most recent messages" vs a time window, whose read-forward caveat is stated), zoom is anchored on the pointer and the reset button **fits the graph to the viewport** instead of returning to a fixed `translate(40,40) scale(1)` that left half a seven-topic chain off screen. The trace **streams**: the page consumes `POST /api/stream-flow/stream` with `fetch` + a `ReadableStream` (not `EventSource`, which only speaks GET and silently reconnects — on a network blip mid-scan it would restart a whole-cluster trace), so hops appear on the graph as they are found, a progress bar states `42/250 topics · 12 800 messages · 3 matches`, and **Stop keeps what was found** instead of discarding it. `parseSseBuffer` is a pure, unit-tested frame reader (events split across chunks, multi-line `data:`, CRLF, comments); a browser without a readable body falls back to the non-streaming endpoint. The **unrun criterion survives leaving the page** too (`readTraceParamsDraft` / `saveTraceParamsDraft`, cleared as soon as the form is emptied): a URL that already describes a trace always wins, or a shared link would overwrite its recipient's form. The criterion is **shareable and replayable**: the whole form round-trips through the query string (`?key=&path=&topics=&window=&max=&regex=&case=&headers=`), the URL is rewritten after each run, a shared link reruns itself on open, "Link" copies it, and the last ten traces are kept in `localStorage` (`kse:flow-history`, de-duplicated on the criterion). Hops export to CSV (through `pages/resultExport.ts`) or to JSON carrying the criterion, the coverage and the warnings — a trace pasted into an incident ticket has to say what it covered. Toggles for exact record key (which disables the path field and the regex it contradicts) / regex / case sensitivity / header search sit under the criterion, and the field description states what the typed path will actually do (dot path, JSONPath, XPath or header). **`analyzeChain` reads the chain instead of leaving it to be read**: end-to-end duration, the slowest hop (only from two hops up — on a single hop "slowest" says nothing), the topics that saw the key more than once with the span they saw it over, and the hops that go backwards. They surface as a line above the table, as row badges, and on the graph itself (the slowest edge thickened in amber, a skewed one dashed in red). A negative latency is **rendered**, not hidden behind an em dash: producer clocks set the record timestamps, so it is the one fact that explains a doubtful chain order. Nothing is inferred past the data — the last topic is not called a dead end, since in a complete chain it is simply the destination. Pure logic (validation, path scope, URL round-trip, history, export, response parsing, layout, fit/zoom, formatting) lives in `pages/streamFlow.ts`.
  **A completed trace is kept as an observation, not only as a criterion** (`pages/flowChains.ts`, `kse:flow-chains`): `recordTracedChain` stores the chain — topics in first-sighting order, per-hop latency, occurrences — so the Metrics page can derive KPIs from the path a key really took (see `MetricSuggestionService`). `kse:flow-history` records what was *searched* and would answer nothing about the pipeline. Same three guards as `draftStore`: a versioned envelope (an unknown shape is erased, never guessed at), a seven-day expiry (a chain from three weeks ago describes topics that may be gone) and five entries, de-duplicated **on the route** — retracing the same key an hour later describes the same pipeline, and keeping both would give two copies of one proposal rather than two proposals. A trace that touched a single topic writes nothing: one sighting is not a path.
  **Ergonomics of the working screen**, all of it driven from that same pure module: the criteria panel **collapses** to a rail (state in `localStorage`, `kse:flow-panel`, `readPanelOpen` — collapsed by default under 1024 px) and the graph refits to the width it gains, while the summary pill keeps the traced key on screen; the panel's action bar is sticky, so `Trace Flow` never falls below a list of topic chips. The graph answers the **keyboard and the finger**: the SVG is focusable (arrows pan, `+`/`−` zoom, `0` fits, `Escape` deselects), each node is a `role="button"` reachable by Tab, panning uses *pointer* events with `touch-action: none` (a tablet could not pan at all before), and a click on the background deselects. Selection is symmetric — a node selected on the graph scrolls its evidence row into view, a row selected in the table brings the node to the centre **only if it was off screen** (`centerOn` / `isNodeVisible`, scale untouched). The overlays are one flex bar rather than four floating boxes, so the cancellation notice no longer covers the scan summary; the amber and dashed-red edges get a legend. A form edited after a run marks the graph **stale** (`sameCriterion`) with a chip that reruns on click, and `Link` / `CSV` / `JSON` describe the *displayed* run (`ranParams`), never the edited form. "No flow found" is actionable: `suggestWidenings` turns the advice into at most four one-click reruns (drop the path, search headers, drop the window, read 1000/topic, ignore case, scan every topic), each changing one thing and stating it. The evidence table gets a filter past six hops (`filterHits`, hop numbers preserved), and its topic link carries the criterion into the Topic Explorer (`buildTopicSearchQuery`). A run is stamped with a generation number: what comes back from a superseded pass — its "trace cancelled" notice, its `loading: false` — is dropped instead of landing on the run that replaced it. A trace stopped by its budget offers to **continue** rather than to start over (`buildContinuation` / `describeContinuation`, in the coverage strip and first in the "no flow found" panel — finishing the scan beats widening a criterion that was never fully applied): only the unread topics are rescanned, the hops already found travel back to be merged server-side, and the displayed criterion, the URL and the history stay those of the whole trace, since the narrowed topic list is a means, not the search. The label states the real scope (`Continue on 2 of the 412 topics never read`) because the server bounds the names it returns.
  **Two traces compare side by side** (`compareFlows` / `describeComparison` / `slowestDivergence`): "ORD-42 arrived, ORD-43 vanished — where?" is the incident question, and it used to take two tabs and two tables read in parallel. `Compare` replays a criterion from the history through the **non-streaming** endpoint (a comparison has nothing to show before it is complete) and puts the two chains in one table: hop number on each side, the per-hop latency on each side, and their difference. What is compared is **hop latency, never absolute timestamps** — two keys processed an hour apart have nothing to say to each other in the absolute, while "this hop took 200 ms there and 4 minutes here" is the finding. A topic only one side saw is badged `not seen`, drawn dashed-amber on the graph (which keeps showing A) and named in the legend; `sameRoute` refuses to call two *empty* traces identical, since that would be a conclusion drawn from no data. A new trace clears the comparison rather than leaving two unrelated criteria side by side.
  **Target topics take a list or a pattern** (`parseTopicList` / `expandTopicPatterns`): commas, spaces and newlines all separate, so a list pasted from a runbook goes in at once, and `orders.*` expands over the catalogue `Layout` already polls — no extra request. `*` is the only wildcard and the dot is literal. A pattern matching nothing is **reported** instead of being sent as a topic name, which would only produce an "unknown topic" warning at the far end of a scan; a plain name that the catalogue does not know is still accepted, since a topic can exist before the 30 s cache shows it.
  **Reading the evidence**: the table's columns **sort** (`sortHits` — chain order stays the default, since it is the meaning of the trace, and a hop's rank comes from `hopNumber`, never from its row position, so it survives sorting and filtering alike); a hop with no latency, the first one, always sorts **last** whichever way the column goes, because asking for the slowest hop and reading an em dash at the top answers nothing. The graph/evidence split is **draggable** (`clampEvidencePct`, `kse:flow-evidence`, 15–75 %, also settable from the keyboard — it is a `role="separator"`), where it used to be a fixed 45 % that left a fifteen-hop chain scrolling inside a slot while four nodes kept the top half. "Max messages / topic" pairs the slider with a `NumberInput`, since 750 was otherwise a matter of hitting one pixel. A finished run is announced in a visually hidden live region: the progress bar is a `status` while it runs, but its disappearance tells a screen-reader user nothing
- `DataModel` (`/data-model`) — generates an entity-relation diagram from a selection of topics (`POST /api/data-model`): each node is a table card listing the inferred columns (key columns first — they carry the edges — capped at 12 with a counted overflow), each edge is styled by its confidence grade and labelled with the referencing column, and the inspector shows the full column list plus every relation **with the evidence it rests on**. Same graph gestures as Lineage (pointer pan, wheel zoom, arrows/`+`/`−`/`0`/`Escape` on the focusable SVG, background click deselects). The selection round-trips through the URL (`?topics=`, rewritten after each run, self-write guarded) so a model is shareable and replays on open; a selection edited after a run marks the model stale rather than letting the graph pass for current, and the coverage line plus the server's warnings say what the model does *not* cover. **The diagram reads like an ER diagram, not like a generic graph**: each edge is anchored on the *row of the column that carries it* (`computeEdgeGeometry` / `columnRowY`) rather than on the node's centre — so an edge leaves `order_id` and lands on the target's key row, which is what makes the link legible without reading the label — and cardinality is drawn in crow's-foot notation (many side on the referencing entity, bar on the referenced one), which frees the line style to mean confidence and nothing else. Edges sharing an anchor are spread (`anchorSpread`) instead of stacking, a column folded into the `+N more` overflow falls back to the node centre rather than pointing at a row that is not on screen, and the graph is **fitted to the viewport** after each run and on the reset button (`graphBounds` / `fitTransform`) — a reset to a fixed `scale(1)` left a fifteen-table model half off screen. The fit is **capped at `MAX_FIT_SCALE` (1.75), not at 1**: the old ceiling meant a three-table model rendered at its natural size in the middle of a canvas three quarters empty, which is the "the graph doesn't use the space" half of the complaint. `fitTransform` still defaults `maxScale` to 1 so any caller that does not ask sees exactly the old behaviour, and the scale itself is computed by `fitScale`, shared with `chooseNodeSizing` — with one difference that is the whole point of splitting it out: the fit needs the `MIN_FIT_SCALE` floor (0.1), the *comparison* must not have it, or two calibres that both bottom out declare themselves equivalent when one fits in a tenth of the space. `fitTransform` takes an optional `topPadding`, reserving space above the content for the coverage/warnings banner: that banner sits `absolute` over the canvas, so `fitToViewport` used to fit against the SVG's whole rect, and a run with warnings rendered its topmost row under the banner it had no way to make room for. `fitToViewport` measures the banner's live height (it varies with how many warnings it lists) and passes it through; the parameter defaults to the horizontal `padding`, so a fit with nothing to reserve reproduces the old symmetric centring exactly. **That measurement is shared with `chooseNodeSizing`** (`measureTopPadding`, one definition, mirrored into state for the memo that cannot read the DOM): the fit reserved up to ~150 px while the calibre chooser assumed 40, so on a height-bound model a calibre could be elected for a legibility the real fit never gave it — on a nine-spoke star of ten columns at 1280×800 the two assumptions pick the two opposite extremes. The fit keeps measuring live rather than reading the state, because state lags one render and the post-generation fit falls in exactly that render, the one where the warnings have just appeared; the chooser lagging a frame is harmless, since a calibre change refits anyway. **And the fit is recomputed when the available space changes** (`isSignificantResize`): it was recomputed after a generation, on crossing the `lg` threshold, and on the reset button — everything else left it describing a rectangle that no longer existed. Two cases mattered, both measured in a real browser: resizing the window *without* crossing the threshold, and **opening the inspector**, which takes 320 px from the canvas on desktop — the graph then ran 220 px and 280 px past the right edge respectively, the overflow sitting under the panel that had just opened. The refit is skipped when the operator has framed the view themselves (`viewAdjusted`, raised by every pan, zoom, arrow key and centring, cleared by a fit): re-centring under someone who has just zoomed into a table is worse than the off-centring. A sub-pixel or scrollbar-sized change is ignored, and a zero-sized measurement — a hidden tab, a collapsed panel — never reframes, since fitting to zero yields an absurd transform that would still be there on return. Entity headers are tinted by **topic domain** (`topicDomains`, the first segment after dropping the leading segments *every* topic shares, so `demo.orders.*` and `demo.payments.*` are two families rather than one `demo`), with the legend derived from what is drawn. **The confidence legend is also the filter** (`filterRelations`), one list rather than two of the same three grades: a model rich in name-only `MEDIUM` matches drowns the `HIGH` edges the target's own key actually supports, so each grade is a checkbox carrying its count. It is a *reading* control and touches exactly one thing — which edges are drawn. The layout and the connected/isolated split stay computed over **all** relations, deliberately: toggling a grade must hide lines, not rearrange the diagram under the operator, and "No deduced relation (N)" has to keep meaning *none* rather than "none among the ones you are looking at". The inspector likewise lists every relation of an entity — it is the evidence panel — but **marks** the ones the filter hides, since a relation listed with no line on screen otherwise explains nothing. Both exports follow: the SVG serialises the DOM so it is already filtered, Mermaid is handed the filtered relations so two exports of one screen cannot disagree, the confidence legend drops the grades that are off, and `exportNotes` states how many edges the filter removed — a detached diagram that does not say what it omits reads as the whole model. **A column that reads as a foreign key but yielded no relation is flagged** (`orphanKeyColumns`, a `?` marker on the node and a named callout in the inspector): it was indistinguishable from an ordinary column, so a diagram that looked incomplete never said why. **The judgement is the server's and travels in `DataModelColumn.keyBase`** — the entity word a name points at, `null` when it points at nobody. It was first mirrored in the browser, and the mirror was wrong in a way the server is not: `DataModelService.referencingBase` also rejects a name echoing its *own* topic (`order_id` on an orders topic is identity, not reference — the same rule `deduceRelations` applies before looking for a target), so the browser copy flagged such a column as a broken key. `referencingBase` is now the single rule both the deduction and the marker read; the frontend keeps only "no relation came out of it". That also collapses the explanation to **one** sentence: a target present in the model always yields a relation, so a base with no reference means the topic is simply not in the selection — which was previously a browser-side check with a second sentence for a case that could not occur. A key-like column is ranked with the keys in `displayedColumns`, not just marked: it carried no `references`, so on a nested payload it fell into the `+N more` overflow exactly when the card is crowded and the marker matters most. **An edge states its evidence where the edge is** — hovering *or* focusing one (it is a Tab-reachable `role="button"` with a transparent 14 px hit path, since a 1.5 px line cannot be caught with a mouse) opens a tooltip carrying the relation, its grade and the server's own `reason`; that justification used to be reachable only by opening a node's inspector, and an explanation only the mouse can reach is not an explanation. Message counts are compacted in the fixed-width header (`formatCount`) with the exact value on the node's SVG `<title>` and in its accessible name. **Entities no relation touches are set aside, not drawn** (`drawnEntities`, over `splitByConnectivity`): they dilute the signal, which is the relations, and — the part that costs the most — each one adds a cell to the grid under the graph, so it lowers the fit scale of *everything else*. They live in a **bounded listbox** (`role="listbox"`, capped height, open by default since that is where they now are) headed "No deduced relation (N)" and captioned by `describeSetAside`, which says why they have no edge rather than leaving a bare count to read as a truncation — and **filtered past `SET_ASIDE_FILTER_MIN` (8)** by `filterEntities`, since fifty entities with no relation is an ordinary outcome on a large selection and the list scrolls well before that; below the threshold a filter is noise. It carries the same grammar as the topic filter higher in the same panel (substring, or an anchored pattern as soon as there is a `*`) because two adjacent fields cannot answer the same text differently, and it matches **the table name or the topic**, both being on screen — the row shows the Flink identifier and carries the topic in its tooltip. `describeSetAsideFilter` says what was kept and lets zero read as zero — each one still selectable and inspectable, with an opt-in to draw them anyway, and a `N not drawn` chip beside the coverage line, because an entity missing from the diagram must say so. **And past `SET_ASIDE_GROUP_MIN` (5) it is grouped by topic family** (`groupSetAside`), because the question a long list of set-aside entities raises is not *which ones* but *which ones together* — "the whole `iot` family is outside the model" is a finding, twelve monospace rows are not. The split is the one the header tints already use, and the `topicDomains` map is **passed in rather than recomputed**: it is built over *every* topic of the model, dropping the leading segments they all share, so recomputing it on the set-aside subset alone would name the same families differently — two answers to "what family is this topic in?" on one screen. It groups **what is displayed**, so the header counts describe the screen by construction, where grouping the whole list and rendering the filtered one would put "iot (12)" above three rows. And it returns `null` rather than a single group whenever grouping teaches nothing — below the threshold, with one family (the header would repeat the section title), or when every family holds one entity (each header names only its own row) — which keeps that judgement in one place instead of leaving the page to refuse a group it was handed. A group is a `role="group"` inside the listbox named by `describeSetAsideGroup`, the visible header being `aria-hidden` so the domain and its count are not read twice; ungrouped, the wrapper is presentational and the options stay direct children of the listbox, so there is one row template either way. The rule left the page's ternary for `drawnEntities` because it decides half the available space; it keeps one exception, and only one: when *no* relation was deduced at all the grid **is** the diagram, since setting everything aside would leave a blank canvas after a successful generation. Selecting one yields **no dimming at all** rather than dimming the whole diagram to announce a node that is not in it. A field-highlight box lights the matching column in every entity that carries it (`matchingColumns`, a case-insensitive substring of the full path, so `customer.id` answers to either half) and says what it found — "who else carries this key?" is the question this diagram raises, and the answer is already in the browser, so it costs no request. **A build says what it is doing, and refuses to invent a percentage** (`describeBuildProgress`): `POST /api/data-model` is one request that answers when every topic is inferred, so the browser cannot know which topic is in flight — a progress bar derived from elapsed time would be a fabricated measurement, which is what this page refuses everywhere else. What is true is the topic count and the elapsed time, and those are what the overlay shows. It matters because the canvas was otherwise **blank for minutes** on a first run, and worse on a second: the previous model stayed on screen with nothing saying so, which `describeStaleGraphDuringBuild` now states. The per-topic budget is deliberately not quoted — it is a server constant, and a UI that copies it lies the day it changes. **A minimap appears only when the graph overflows the viewport** (`minimapLayout` / `visibleGraphRect` / `graphFullyVisible`): with everything already on screen it would draw a frame around its own box — an ornament that teaches nothing and costs a corner of the canvas. Clicking it moves the view (`centerOnGraphPoint`, current scale kept), which is the one gesture an overview owes: it situates, and it takes you where you looked. **A subgraph opens as one query** (`buildMultiJoinSql`): `buildJoinSql` answers "open this relation", but re-joining three tables by hand is the real gesture on a star schema, and the diagram already knows every predicate. Entities are added to a join set from the inspector; the query is built from a **breadth-first spanning tree** so every `JOIN` predicate cites a table already introduced — one that names a table further down does not parse. It **refuses rather than inventing a predicate**, the same rule as the binary version and it says which entity is unreachable: a set the deduced relations do not connect has no join, and fabricating one would assert an equality nothing supports. `joinAliasesFor` is the N-ary alias rule, and `joinAliases` now delegates to it — two ways of naming the same tables drift, which is a defect this module's test caught once already. **Named selections are kept by the browser** (`readSavedModels` / `saveModel` / `deleteSavedModel`, `kse:data-model-saved`): the URL is shareable but anonymous, and nothing in it says this is *the* order pipeline. Same shape as `flowChains` — versioned envelope erased rather than guessed at, bounded entries, best-effort writes — with **one deliberate difference: no expiry**. A draft is an accident of navigation and is right to forget after a week; a named save is a deliberate gesture, and dropping it unasked is data loss. Saving under an existing name replaces it in place, since stacking homonyms makes the list unusable. **Finding a table by name doesn't need the mouse**: a "Jump to an entity" combobox (`centerOnEntity`) centres the viewport on a chosen entity at a readable scale — raised to a floor if the graph is currently zoomed out further, left alone otherwise, since re-zooming a table already close enough would undo what the operator just did by hand. Restricted to *drawn* entities — one folded into "No deduced relation" has no position to centre on, and that list already exists to select it. **Comparing two selections has no server-side history to read**: unlike the audit, a data model is never persisted, so "compare" means two independent `POST /api/data-model` calls and a diff computed client-side (`diffModels`) — the same choice Stream Flow's Compare page makes for a trace. Relations carry no stable id across two runs (they're recomputed each time), so the diff pairs them by what they describe — the two columns they connect (`relationKey`) — not by position in the array; the same pair at a different confidence is one changed relation, not a removed one and an added one, because it's the same claim better or worse supported the second time. The result is a list, not two overlapping graphs redrawn on top of each other: past a handful of entities two superimposed diagrams read as noise, where "+2 entities, 1 relation added, 1 confidence changed" reads as an answer. **The diagram exports as a standalone SVG or PNG** (`buildExportSvg`), and the file carries the coverage line, both legends and what is *not* drawn (`exportNotes`) — a diagram pasted into a ticket cannot be interrogated, so one that does not state its bounds reads as a complete model, the same rule as Stream Flow's JSON export. The markup comes from the live DOM rather than being re-emitted: two renderers for one image drift, and the export would stop showing what the screen shows. **But it is serialised at the widest calibre, not the screen's** — the screen's is chosen for *a viewport*, and on a large model it folds columns into `+N more` so the diagram fits; a file has no viewport (an SVG zooms without end, the PNG is rasterised at 2×), so the export was freezing `+14 more columns` for ever, on the one artefact whose reader can zoom. `exportDiagram` therefore forces `COMFORTABLE_NODE_SIZING` across two synchronous `flushSync` commits and restores it in a `finally`: still one renderer and still the live DOM, simply not at the size of the screen, and both commits land in one task so nothing flashes. The layout it measures is recomputed from the same pure functions rather than read from the memo, which still holds the screen's. Mermaid needs none of this and returns before it — it re-emits from the response and has always carried every column. The selection is cleared through `flushSync` first, since exporting it would bake in the dimming and hand back a three-quarters-erased image; the PNG is rasterised at 2× through a canvas and a failure there points at the SVG (which works) instead of vanishing. **Topics are chosen the way Stream Flow chooses them** (`topicSelection.ts`): a name, a list pasted from a runbook or a `kafka-topics --list`, or a pattern `demo.orders.*` expanded over the catalogue `Layout` already polls — the same `parseTopicList` / `expandTopicPatterns`, imported rather than restated, since two list splitters that drift are two behaviours for one gesture. The checkbox list stays beside it for browsing, and **its filter understands the same patterns** (`filterTopics` → `matchesTopicPattern`): the field above it advertises `demo.orders.*` in its placeholder, and typing that into the filter returned nothing at all — which reads as "no such topic", not as "wrong syntax". Two adjacent fields on one panel, one of which teaches a syntax, cannot answer the same text differently. Plain text stays a substring, which is what a filter is; a `*` switches to the selection's own anchored rule, so the filter shows exactly what the field would add. The matcher is case-insensitive like the substring path has always been — safe, since a pattern is only ever matched against topics that exist, so it cannot invent one. Process Mining had the same defect with its own inlined copy of the filter, and now calls the same function. **A pattern that finds nothing names the form that would** (`suggestBroaderPattern`): patterns are anchored, which is what makes them precise — `demo.*` does not drag in `internal.demo.x` — but the cost is that `orders*` matches nothing on a `demo.orders.…` cluster and an empty result reads as "no such topic". The escape hatch already existed (`*orders*` works), so what was missing was knowing it: the toast and the filter both say *try `*orders*`*, and only when broadening would actually match. Broadening automatically was the wrong fix and was rejected — it would dilute every other pattern's precision to offer what the syntax already gives. Two rules are its own: the topic cap applies — **a `Max topics` field on the panel** now, bounded by the ceiling `GET /api/data-model/limits` reports (`clampMaxTopics`, which assumes the *default* while the limits are unknown rather than a more permissive server: a generation refused after minutes of inference is the worse of the two outcomes) — and what it refuses is **reported** rather than silently truncated — including on a URL-carried selection (`capTopics`), which had no cap of its own: a shared link naming more than the cap passed it straight to the server, and the checkbox list came back with every box locked (`full` reads off `selection.length >= maxTopics`) with nothing on screen saying why. `capTopics` trims it and the overflow is named in a toast, the same contract `describeTopicEntry` already gives a typed list — and an explicitly typed `internal.*` is honoured where `selectAll` skips those — ticking "everything shown" is a bulk gesture over a filtered list, while a typed pattern is a deliberate request. A pattern matching nothing is named instead of being sent as a topic name, and a plain name the catalogue does not know is still accepted, since a topic can exist before the 30 s cache shows it. **The budget travels with the selection, everywhere the selection does** — in the URL (`maxTopicsFromQuery` / `buildQuery`, the parameter omitted when it equals the default so already-shared links are unchanged), in the draft and in a named save. It did not, and that was a defect introduced by making it adjustable at all: a link describing a fifty-topic generation opened at the recipient's default of 30, `capTopics` dropped twenty, and a toast announced they were "left out" — of a model that had in fact been built on all fifty. The same loss happened on a plain reload and on reopening a saved selection. `SelectionDraft` and `SavedModel` both carry it as `number | null`, and both **read back an entry written before it existed** rather than discarding it — a draft is precisely what must not be lost across a version. A URL-carried budget also bounds the replay itself, which is why `requestTimeoutMs` needs the limits *before* the replay runs: `limitsSettled` gates it, because a shared link to a large model is exactly the case the derived wait exists for and it was the one case still getting the floor. **The unrun selection survives leaving the page** (`readSelectionDraft` / `saveSelectionDraft`, on `draftStore` like everywhere else): the URL carries an *executed* selection — it is rewritten after each generation — so choosing fifteen topics, going to check a field name in the Topic Explorer and coming back handed back an empty form. Exactly the gesture Stream Flow was fixed for, and the same rule: a URL that describes a selection always wins, or a shared link would overwrite its recipient's form. The cap applies on read, and a draft written by an older version is filtered to strings rather than trusted. **Column names are shortened from the left** (`shortenColumnName`): nested paths keep their dot notation, so cutting from the right gives `shipping.address.ci…` and amputates precisely the part that tells one column of a sub-object from another — three fields of one object become three identical rows. The prefix is what repeats on a card, so the prefix is what is elided (`…address.city`), with the full path on the row's `<title>`; a name with no dot is still cut from the right, having no redundant part. **A relation opens as a query** (`buildJoinSql`): a `HIGH` relation *is* a join predicate — `payments.order_id = orders.order_id` — and this diagram is the only place in the application where that predicate is already known, so each relation card carries a link into the SQL editor through the `?sql=` it already accepts. No `SELECT *` (both sides carry the join column, so the star yields two homonyms), nested paths left out of the projection with a caveat saying how many, the evidence and the unbounded-join warning carried as comments in the SQL itself rather than in a tooltip nobody exports, and a `LIMIT` so the editor gets a query that terminates. It **refuses rather than inventing a predicate**: a `MEDIUM` relation onto an entity with no detected key describes no join, and guessing one would be the assumption-presented-as-fact this diagram avoids everywhere else. Aliases come from the topics' distinctive segments (`joinAliases`), falling back to the last segment for sibling topics — `demo.orders.1.received` and `demo.orders.2.validated` differ only by a digit, and an alias starting with one is not an SQL identifier, which is a defect its own test found. A third export is **Mermaid `erDiagram`** (`toMermaidEr`), the only textual one, and it exists for what the two images cannot do: **be re-read and diffed** — a PNG in a review does not say what changed since the previous version. The confidence travels in the relation label rather than a comment, because Mermaid has no per-edge line style and a diagram that dropped the grade would present a guess and a key match identically; identifiers are sanitised for Mermaid's syntax with the original name kept as a column comment. **Under the `lg` threshold both side panels become overlay drawers** (`useIsDesktop`): fixed, they cost 576 px of chrome on a viewport that has fewer than 1024, leaving the canvas — the one thing this page exists to show — a few dozen pixels wide, which is the defect `MOBILE-LAYOUT-SCOPE.md` documents for the SQL editor. Crossing the threshold re-fits the graph, since the previous framing describes a viewport that no longer exists. **On desktop the selection panel folds to a rail** (`readPanelOpen` / `writePanelOpen`, `kse:data-model-panel`, the same mechanic as Stream Flow's criteria panel under its own key): it costs 256 px permanently to the only thing this page exists to show, and once the model is generated it does nothing but regenerate it. Folding clears `viewAdjusted` so the graph reclaims the width instead of keeping a framing computed for a rectangle 256 px narrower — a gesture whose whole purpose *is* the graph's room is the one case where the page takes the framing back.
  **The box size is chosen, not constant** (`chooseNodeSizing`, `NodeSizing`, bounded by `MIN_NODE_W` 160 / `MAX_NODE_W` 300). It was six module constants, so a two-entity model and a hundred-entity one got identical 230 px boxes with twelve rows — and that constant silently decided whether the diagram could be read at all: comfortable at three tables, fitted at scale 0.25 at fifty, where no column name resolves. Three calibres (comfortable / default / compact) are each laid out and fitted for real — the layout is O(n) and the model is capped at 100 entities, so three passes cost nothing next to what they prevent — and the first whose **rendered** text (`sizing.rowSize × fit scale`) clears `MIN_READABLE_TEXT_PX` wins; if none does, the one that renders largest. That is the whole trade: a narrower box listing fewer columns shrinks the graph's extent, which *raises* the fit scale, so the text on screen ends up bigger than a large box seen too far away. The default calibre reproduces the old constants exactly and is what every signature takes by default, so `NODE_W` / `HEADER_H` / `ROW_H` / `FOOTER_H` / `MAX_COLUMNS_SHOWN` are now derived from it rather than being the source. A calibre change refits **without consulting `viewAdjusted`**, unlike a resize: every node moved, so the old framing describes a drawing that no longer exists. **And it is a default, not a verdict** (`DensityChoice`, `resolveNodeSizing`): the automatic pick optimises for legibility, which is right most of the time and is not always what is wanted — looking for one column across fifty tables means seeing them all and zooming, and the automatism answered "six of twenty" with no recourse. A `Box size` select overrides it, `describeDensity` states what `auto` elected (otherwise the word says nothing), and `describeDensityCost` names what the current calibre costs when its text renders under `MIN_READABLE_TEXT_PX` — said whatever the choice, since `auto` falls under the floor too on a model where no calibre clears it, and it is a fact about the screen rather than a reproach. Deliberately **not persisted**, unlike the panel fold: the right calibre depends on the model on screen, so a stored `comfortable` would one day open a hundred-entity model and make it unreadable with nothing explaining why — and `auto` already serves whoever prefers compact, since it elects it as soon as the model asks.
  **A crowded layer wraps into adjacent sub-columns** (`maxNodesPerColumn`). A hub thirty topics reference — the most ordinary shape a data model takes — put all thirty in one column: an extent of 424 × 5 099 px, which *by construction* can never fill a screen, so the fit was height-bound at 0.14 with two thirds of the width empty. Measured on a 24-spoke star at 1280 × 800, wrapping takes the fit from 0.38 to 0.92 and the rendered text from 3.4 px to 8.3 px. The accepted cost is that an edge entering the second sub-column passes *behind* the first's nodes (edges are painted before nodes), and on a thirty-edge fan into one hub no layout separates them anyway. `orphanColumns` widens the isolated grid on the same reasoning, instead of staying four wide whatever its population. What neither fixes is a deep chain — twelve topics end to end are 3 000 px wide however they are drawn — which is what the minimap and zoom are for.
  Pure logic (selection, URL, node sizing and calibre choice, layered layout with real node heights and wrapped layers, edge geometry, cardinality glyphs, fit, domains, connectivity split, count compaction, field search, export framing, Mermaid emission, coverage line) lives in `pages/dataModel.ts`, unit-tested
- `Lineage` (`/lineage`) — interactive dependency graph (custom SVG; no external graph lib). Same interaction model as the Stream Flow graph, and for the same reasons: panning uses *pointer* events with `touch-action: none` (a tablet could not pan at all), the SVG is focusable with arrows to pan, `+`/`−` to zoom, `0` to reset and `Escape` to deselect, every node is a Tab-reachable `role="button"` that opens its details on Enter or Space (and shows its tooltip on focus, not only on hover), and clicking the background deselects. Statements the backend could not resolve are listed above the graph — see `LineageService`
- **A card carries the measurement and the rule it calls for, not only a number** (`pages/metricScope.ts`'s `describeMeasurement` / `describeRefreshCost`, `pages/metricAlert.ts`, both pure + tested). Three gaps, and they are one gap: everything this page computes was being shown to a scraper rather than to the person looking at it. `MetricCard` rendered `lastValue` and nothing else — on an écart **`5` says nothing and `12 against 7` is the diagnostic**, and both sides were in `lastSummary`, computed, persisted, rendered nowhere; on a latency it is worse since the p95 became a Prometheus series, because it then travelled to a scraper and not to the card. `describeMeasurement` returns the components the server actually measured — never a derivation, an absent key producing no part and a **zero producing one, since zero is a measurement**. **The threshold had one direction**: `getStatus` knew only `>=` and `validateThresholds` *refused* `warn >= crit` outright, so the descending reading was unreachable — a `RATIO` is healthy at 1.0 and breaks by falling, which is the 0.99-then-0.95 pair that rule forbade. The direction is now **derived from the order of the two thresholds rather than from a field of its own** (`thresholdDirection`): critical is always worse than warning, which is the one thing the pair is known to say, so a critical below the warning reads the metric downwards. No component on `MetricConfig`, none of its forty-three construction sites touched, and the convention is checkable by looking at two numbers — on the condition that it is **displayed**, which `describeThresholdDirection` does under the fields and the card's `≤` / `≥` sign follows; only equality stays an error, two identical thresholds expressing no direction and firing together. And **the page stopped one step before what these metrics are for**: it all exists so an alert fires, and the PromQL was written nowhere — which stopped being obvious the day the companion series arrived, since a frozen gauge fires exactly like a real breach. `buildAlertRule` emits it under two rules: **the alert must compare what the card compares**, so a `GAUGE` gets a rule while a `COUNTER` (whose series accumulates deltas, not this number) and a `HISTOGRAM`/`SUMMARY` (a distribution) get the reason there is none, naming what *is* exported; and every rule carries `explorer_metric_last_success_timestamp_seconds`, with a window of four times the metric's own `refreshIntervalMs` — or 120 s, **said to be an assumption** when the cadence is the loop's. On a transit latency the p95 series is offered as a second rule rather than substituted, since the threshold was set against an average and a p95 is above one by construction.
